Specifically, suppose there are nontrivial amplifications at stages
1\leq s_{1}\leq\cdots\leq s_{l}\leq m
, with the corresponding amplification step numbers
r_{s_{1}},\ldots,r_{s_{l}}\geq 1
. Then, the cost of nested amplitude amplification previously stated in
Eq.

46
should be revised to

\mathbf{Cost}(\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle)=\mathbf{Cost}(|\psi_{0}\rangle)\prod_{u=1}^{l}(2r_{s_{u}}+1)+\sum_{v=1}^{l+1}\mathbf{Cost}(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1})\prod_{u=v}^{l}(2r_{s_{u}}+1),

(58)

where we adopt the convention that
s_{0}=0
,
s_{l+1}=m+1
and
A_{m+1}=I
. That is, the query cost is multiplied by
2r_{s_{u}}+1
only at the
l
nontrivial stages, and we merge the cost of input algorithms
\mathbf{Cost}(A_{k})
for
k=s_{v-1}+1,\ldots,s_{v}
, associated with the same query product
\prod_{u=v}^{l}(2r_{s_{u}}+1)
.

Let us consider a general query product from stage
s_{v}
to
s_{w}
and re-express it as

\displaystyle\prod_{u=v}^{w}(2r_{s_{u}}+1)=\prod_{k=s_{v}}^{s_{w}}(2r_{k}+1)

(59)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=s_{v}}^{s_{w}}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{(2r_{k}+1)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\widetilde{A}_{k-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}}\right)\prod_{k=s_{v}}^{s_{w}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\widetilde{A}_{k-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=s_{v}}^{s_{w}}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{(2r_{k}+1)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\widetilde{A}_{k-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}}\right)\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{w}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{w}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v}-1}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v}-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v}-1}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v}-1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{{s_{w}}}\Pi_{b}}A_{{s_{w}}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}

\displaystyle=\prod_{u=v}^{w}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{u}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{u}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{(2r_{s_{u}}+1)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{u}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{u}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{u}-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}}\right)\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{w}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{w}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v-1}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{w}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{w}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}.

Here, the first equality uses the observation that for
s_{v}\leq k\leq s_{w}
, either we have a nontrivial stage with
k=s_{u}
for
v\leq u\leq w
, or we have a trivial stage with
r_{k}=0
. The second equality is a direct rewriting. The third equality can be reasoned using
Eq.

44
in a similar way as
[
12
, Lemma 16]
:

\displaystyle\prod_{k=s_{v}}^{s_{w}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\widetilde{A}_{k-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=s_{v}}^{s_{w}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k-1}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k-1}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\widetilde{A}_{k-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\right)

(60)

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=s_{v}}^{s_{w}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k-1}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k-1}\Pi_{b}}A_{k-1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{w}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{w}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v}-1}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v}-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v}-1}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v}-1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{{s_{w}}}\Pi_{b}}A_{{s_{w}}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}.

The justification of the last equality requires some more efforts. In the first factor, we have changed the multiplication variable from
k
back to
u
, using again the observation that for
s_{v}\leq k\leq s_{w}
, either we have a nontrivial stage with
k=s_{u}
for
v\leq u\leq w
, or we have a trivial stage with
r_{k}=0
and loss factor
\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{k}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{(2r_{k}+1)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\widetilde{A}_{k-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}=1
.
Now recall that
s_{v-1}+1,\ldots,s_{v}-1
are all trivial stages by definition.
Thus, invoking
Eq.

44
,

\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v}-1}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v}-1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v}-1}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v}-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}=\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v}-1}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v}-1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v}-1}\Pi_{b}}{A}_{s_{v}-1}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v-1}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}=\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v-1}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}.

(61)

This proves the claimed representation of query product.
As this representation may be of independent interest, let us encapsulate it into a lemma.

Lemma 5

(Representation of query product)
.

Let
\left(\{\Pi_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m},\Pi_{b},\{A_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m},\{r_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m},|\psi_{0}\rangle\right)
be a variable time nested amplitude amplification according to
Definition
2
. Suppose that
r_{k}\geq 1
happens only at
l
stages
1\leq s_{1}\leq\cdots\leq s_{l}\leq m
.
Then,

\displaystyle\prod_{u=v}^{w}(2r_{s_{u}}+1)=\prod_{u=v}^{w}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{u}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{u}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{(2r_{s_{u}}+1)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{u}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{u}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{u}-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}}\right)\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{w}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{w}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v-1}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{w}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{w}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}

(62)

for
1\leq v\leq w\leq l
, under the convention that
s_{0}=0
.

We now apply this representation to the query products in
Eq.

58
, all of which end with
w=l
.
By definition,
s_{l}
is the last nontrivial stage so
s_{l}+1,\ldots,m
are all trivial.
Invoking
Eq.

44
once more,

\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{l}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{l}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{l}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{l}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}=\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}A_{m}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}A_{m}\cdots A_{s_{l}+1}\widetilde{A}_{s_{l}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}=\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}A_{m}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert},

(63)

giving

\displaystyle\prod_{u=v}^{l}(2r_{s_{u}}+1)=\prod_{u=v}^{l}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{u}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{u}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{(2r_{s_{u}}+1)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{u}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{u}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{u}-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}}\right)\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v-1}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}A_{m}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert}.

(64)

To proceed, note that the first factor is the inverse loss factor, which can be bounded by
\left(\frac{6}{5}\right)^{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}}
. Then, we have the amplitude of post-amplified algorithm trivially bounded by
\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\leq 1
. In practice, we would also adjust the last stage of VTAA so that the amplitude is at least constant. Next,
\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}A_{m}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert=\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}
by definition. We now lower bound
\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v-1}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert
:

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{s_{v-1}}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\sin\left((2r_{s_{v-1}}+1)\arcsin\left(\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\widetilde{A}_{{s_{v-1}}-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\right)\right)

(65)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{5}{6}\right)^{\alpha_{s_{v-1}}}(2r_{s_{v-1}}+1)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\widetilde{A}_{{s_{v-1}}-1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Omega}\left(\sqrt{\alpha_{s_{v-1}}}\right).

This analysis holds for all query products starting at
v=1,\ldots,l
. In particular, for the cost of initial state preparation, we have
v=1
,
\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{0}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{0}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert=\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{0}\Pi_{b}}A_{0}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert=1
.
Altogether, this gives the query complexity of Tunable VTAA

\displaystyle\mathbf{Cost}(\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{O}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}(A_{s_{1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle)

(66)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\sum_{v=2}^{l+1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{s_{v-1}}}}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)\Bigg).

Note that VTAA would have the same cost if we were to pre-merge the input algorithms
A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}
during compilation, resulting in a (
l+1
)-stage algorithm. We now claim that
l=\mathbf{O}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\right)
. Indeed, this follows from the observation that

3^{l}\leq\prod_{u=1}^{l}(2r_{s_{u}}+1)\leq\left(\frac{6}{5}\right)^{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}=\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\quad\Rightarrow\quad l=\mathbf{O}\left(\log_{3}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\right).

(67)

Therefore, in the interesting regime where
\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\ll m
, majority of the algorithms can be pre-merged yielding a significantly simplified VTAA.

3.3
\ell_{\frac{2}{3}}
-quasinorm scaling

Having established the universal property and query complexity of Tunable VTAA, we now explore its limitation. In particular, we will show that complexity of Tunable VTAA can be optimized to scale with
\ell_{\frac{2}{3}}
-quasinorm of the input cost.

To this end, we note that the query cost of initial state preparation and algorithm
A_{s_{1}}\cdots A_{1}
has the scaling
\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)
independent of the amplification schedules, so it suffices to optimize query complexity of the remaining input algorithms. Up to a rescaling, this reduces to solving the following problem:

\displaystyle\mathrm{minimize}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{v=2}^{l+1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{s_{v-1}}}}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)

(68)

\displaystyle\textrm{subject to}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{v=2}^{l+1}\alpha_{s_{v-1}}=1,

\displaystyle\alpha_{s_{v-1}}>0,\quad v=2,\ldots,l+1.

This optimization problem can be readily solved by the weighted mean inequality. Specifically, given positive real numbers
\{w_{v}\}_{v=1}^{l}
and
\{x_{v}\}_{v=1}^{l}
, the inequality asserts that the weighted harmonic mean is always upper bounded by the weighted quadratic mean
[
53
, Problem 8.3]
:

\frac{\sum\limits_{v=1}^{l}w_{v}}{\sum\limits_{v=1}^{l}w_{v}\frac{1}{x_{v}}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\sum\limits_{v=1}^{l}w_{v}x_{v}^{2}}{\sum\limits_{v=1}^{l}w_{v}}}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\sum\limits_{v=1}^{l}w_{v}\frac{1}{x_{v}}\geq\frac{\left(\sum\limits_{v=1}^{l}w_{v}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\sqrt{\sum\limits_{v=1}^{l}w_{v}x_{v}^{2}}}

(69)

with equality if and only if
x_{1}=\cdots=x_{l}
. Specialized to our problem, let us choose

\displaystyle w_{v-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)\right)^{\frac{2}{3}},

(70)

\displaystyle x_{v-1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\alpha_{s_{v}-1}}\left(\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)\right)^{-\frac{1}{3}},

for
v=2,\ldots,l+1
.
Then, the weighted mean inequality implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{v=2}^{l+1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{s_{v-1}}}}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)

(71)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\sum_{v=2}^{l+1}\left(\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}

with the lower bound attained when

\alpha_{s_{v}-1}\propto\left(\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}.

(72)

We have thus established:

Proposition 6

(Query complexity of Tunable VTAA)
.

Let
\left(\{\Pi_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m},\Pi_{b},\{A_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m},\{\alpha_{j}\}_{j=0}^{m},|\psi_{0}\rangle\right)
be a Tunable VTAA according to
Definition
3
. Then,

(i)

Assuming the threshold values satisfy
0\leq\alpha_{j}\leq 1
and
\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}=\mathbf{O}(1)
, nontrivial amplifications happen only at
l
stages
1\leq s_{1}\leq\cdots\leq s_{l}\leq m
, where

l=\mathbf{O}\left(\log_{3}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\right),\qquad p_{\text{succ}}=\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}A_{m}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}.

(73)

Under the convention that
s_{l+1}=m+1
and
A_{m+1}=I
, Tunable VTAA has query complexity

\displaystyle\mathbf{Cost}(\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{O}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}(A_{s_{1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle)

(74)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\sum_{v=2}^{l+1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{s_{v-1}}}}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)\Bigg).

(ii)

Pre-merging trivial stages and using thresholds

\alpha_{s_{v-1}}\propto\left(\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)\right)^{\frac{2}{3}},

(75)

the complexity of Tunable VTAA can be minimized to attain the
\ell_{\frac{2}{3}}
-quasinorm
scaling

\displaystyle\mathbf{Cost}(\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{O}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}(A_{s_{1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle)

(76)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\left(\sum_{v=2}^{l+1}\left(\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{s_{v-1}}\Pi_{b}}A_{s_{v-1}}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{s_{v}}\cdots A_{s_{v-1}+1}\right)\right)^{\frac{2}{3}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\Bigg).

In our above discussion, we have used precise values of all the norms involved to simplify the presentation, but they can be replaced by their constant multiplicative approximators without affecting the asymptotic analysis.
If this prior knowledge is not available, one could perform nested amplitude estimation like in
[
2
,
12
]
, introducing logarithmic overhead and substantially complicating the structure of algorithm.
However, the advantage of using Tunable VTAA is that this nested amplitude estimation may be completely avoided if we choose the threshold values analytically. We will demonstrate this feature in
Section
4
for solving the quantum linear system problem.

4
Discretized inverse state

In this section, we introduce a
discretized inverse state
for solving the quantum linear system problem, which can be efficiently prepared by Tunable VTAA. We begin with its construction in
Section
4.1
by performing
Gapped Phase Estimation
(GPE) on quantum walk, taking special care of the issue that eigenphases of the walk operator are split into two branches with opposite
\pm
signs. We then present a deterministic amplification schedule in
Section
4.2
that significantly simplifies the structure of VTAA.

To further reduce the query complexity, we present an improved analysis of VTAA in
Section
4.3
by projecting error onto the potentially good subspaces. Finally, we develop a simple solution norm estimation algorithm in
Section
4.4
to estimate
p_{\text{succ}}
when a constant multiplicative approximation of it is unavailable a prior.

4.1
Gapped phase estimation with branch marking

In the quantum linear system problem, the coefficient matrix is block encoded by oracle
O_{A}
as
A/\alpha_{A}
with normalization factor
\alpha_{A}\geq\left\lVert A\right\rVert
, and an upper bound on its inverse
\alpha_{A^{-1}}\geq\left\lVert A^{-1}\right\rVert
is known. We now make a few more assumptions to simplify the discussion without affecting generality. First, we assume that
A
is Hermitian and
O_{A}
is a Hermitian unitary. We can always fulfill this requirement by considering the Hermitian dilation
|0\rangle\!\langle 1|\otimes A+|1\rangle\!\langle 0|\otimes A^{\dagger}
block encoded by the Hermitian unitary
|0\rangle\!\langle 1|\otimes O_{A}+|1\rangle\!\langle 0|\otimes O_{A}^{\dagger}
and two identical isometries, along with the corresponding initial state
|0\rangle|b\rangle
. Second, we assume
\alpha_{A}\geq 2\left\lVert A\right\rVert
, which can be achieved by block encoding a constant factor to artificially increase the normalization factor. Third, we assume that both
\alpha_{A}
and
\alpha_{A^{-1}}
are integer powers of
3
. Again the former can be satisfied by rescaling the block encoding, whereas the latter can be realized using a slightly looser bound at most a constant factor
3
larger.
As an immediate consequence, the upper bound
\kappa=\alpha_{A}\alpha_{A^{-1}}
on the condition number is also an integer power of
3
, and thus the number of VTAA stages
m=\log_{3}(\kappa)
is an integer. Moreover, for any eigenvalue
\lambda_{u}
of
A
, the block encoded operator
\frac{A}{\alpha_{A}}
has an eigenvalue
\frac{\lambda}{\alpha_{A}}
such that
\left\lvert\frac{\lambda}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\left[\frac{1}{3^{m}},1\right)
.

Our algorithm proceeds by applying quantum signal processing to the quantum walk operator. Specifically, assume that the coefficient matrix is block encoded as
\frac{A}{\alpha_{A}}=G^{\dagger}O_{A}G
for some isometry
G
. Then, the quantum walk operator is defined by
W=\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)O_{A}
. In
Appendix
C
, we give a self-contained exposition of the qubitization result
[
39
]
linking a block encoding to its walk operator. In particular, we have the following spectrum correspondence: if the input matrix has the spectral decomposition

A=\sum_{u}\lambda_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u}|,

(77)

then the walk operator has the corresponding spectral decomposition

W=\sum_{u}\left(e^{+i\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)}|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y+}|+e^{-i\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)}|\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y-}|\right)

(78)

when restricted to
\mathbf{Im}\left(GG^{\dagger}\right)+\mathbf{Im}\left(O_{A}GG^{\dagger}O_{A}^{\dagger}\right)
, where

|\phi_{u,0}\rangle=G|\phi_{u}\rangle,\qquad|\phi_{u,1}\rangle=\frac{O_{A}G|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}},\qquad|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle=\frac{|\phi_{u,0}\rangle\pm i|\phi_{u,1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}.

(79)

Note that we have omitted the degenerate
1
-dimensional subspaces in qubitization, because all our eigenvalues satisfy
\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert<1
following the rescaling assumption at the beginning of this subsection.

Now, suppose that the initial state can be expanded in the eigenbasis of
A
as
|b\rangle=\sum_{u}\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle
. We can then apply
G
and expand it in the eigenbasis of
W
as

G|b\rangle=\sum_{u}\gamma_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle=\sum_{u}\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u,0}\rangle=\sum_{u}\gamma_{u}\frac{|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle+|\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}.

(80)

Next, we apply GPE to label the interval to which every eigenvalue belongs. In earlier work
[
15
]
, GPE is performed on the time evolution operator
e^{iA}
, which introduces the overhead of Hamiltonian simulation. More recent work
[
13
]
suggested applying GPE directly on the quantum walk operator
W
. In our notation, this would produce an unnormalized state of the form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\Bigg(\left(\zeta_{k+1,u,+}\frac{3^{k+1}}{3^{m}}|k\rangle+\zeta_{k,u,+}\frac{3^{k}}{3^{m}}|k-1\rangle\right)\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle

(81)

\displaystyle+\left(\zeta_{k+1,u,-}\frac{3^{k+1}}{3^{m}}|k\rangle+\zeta_{k,u,-}\frac{3^{k}}{3^{m}}|k-1\rangle\right)\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle\Bigg).

As the coefficients
\zeta_{k,u,+}
are different from
\zeta_{k,u,-}
in general, the original uniform superposition of
|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle
and
|\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle
is “distorted” by GPE into a non-uniform superposition. Moreover, this distortion on the
\pm
branches of quantum walk is not recorded by any ancilla state, and so it is not possible to perform the correct amplitude amplification. This then leads to a highly inaccurate solution state, a critical issue left unattended in
[
13
]
.

We address this issue in
Appendix
D
by a gadget called
branch marking
. Up to a controllable error, branch marking realizes the transformation

|+\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\mapsto|\pm\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle,

(82)

recording information about the signs of eigenphases of the walk operator into an ancilla register. Given thresholds
\gamma,\frac{\gamma}{\rho}
, we then perform GPE controlled by the branch register to transform

|0\rangle|\pm\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\mapsto|\xi_{u}\rangle|\pm\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle,\qquad|\xi_{u}\rangle=\xi_{u,0}|0\rangle+\xi_{u,1}|1\rangle,

(83)

such that

|\xi_{u}\rangle\approx\begin{cases}|0\rangle,\quad&\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in[\gamma,1),\\
i|1\rangle,&\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in\left[-\frac{\gamma}{\rho},\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right],\\
-|0\rangle,&\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in\left(-1,-\gamma\right],\end{cases}

(84)

Importantly, the output state
|\xi_{u}\rangle
has
no
dependence on the specific
\pm
branch of quantum walk, regardless of whether the original eigenvalue
\lambda_{u}
is in the passband, stopband or transition band of GPE—this is the primary purpose of introducing branch marking.

We now describe a variable time quantum algorithm to prepare the discretized inverse state. Our algorithm acts on a clock register
|j\rangle
holding values
j=0,\ldots,m-1
, a two-qubit flag register with possible states

|\mathrm{good}\rangle=|00\rangle,\qquad|\mathrm{bad}\rangle=|10\rangle,\qquad|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle=|01\rangle,

(85)

and a system register holding the solution state and ancilla state for block encoding, a single qubit
|\pm\rangle
for branch marking, and any additional ancillas consumed by the branch marking and GPE. We introduce accuracy parameters
\epsilon_{\text{bm}},\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}
to be specified later. The algorithm is then constructed as follows.

1.

Initialization:

(a)

Set the state of clock register to be
|0\rangle
.

(b)

Set the state of flag register to be
|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle
.

(c)

Apply the branch marking unitary from
Proposition
22
to implement the transformation
|+\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\mapsto|\pm\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle
with accuracy
\epsilon_{\text{bm}}
.

2.

Discretized inversion: for
j=1,\ldots,m-1
,

(a)

Controlled on the clock state
|j-1\rangle
, flip the second qubit of the flag register and apply GPE from
Proposition
23
with
\gamma=\frac{1}{3^{j}}
,
\rho=3
and accuracy
\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}
, storing outcome back to the second qubit of flag register. Up to error
\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}
, this implements the transformation
|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle|\pm\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\mapsto\left(\xi_{j,u,0}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\xi_{j,u,1}|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle\right)|\pm\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle
, where
\xi_{j,u,1}=0
if
\frac{1}{3^{j}}\leq\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert<\frac{1}{2}
, and
\xi_{j,u,0}=0
if
0\leq\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert<\frac{1}{3^{j+1}}
. Note that both
\pm
branches pick up the same coefficients
\xi_{j,u,0}
and
\xi_{j,u,1}
in the outcome state.

(b)

Controlled on the state
|j-1\rangle
, implement the mapping
|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle\mapsto|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle,|\mathrm{good}\rangle\mapsto\frac{3^{j}}{3^{m}}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\sqrt{1-\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}}|\mathrm{bad}\rangle
. This can in turn be achieved by applying a Pauli-
Y
rotation on the first qubit of flag register, controlled by the second qubit in state
|0\rangle
.

(c)

Controlled on the flag state
|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle
, increment the clock state
|j\rangle\mapsto|j+1\rangle
.

3.

Finalization:

(a)

Controlled on the clock state
|m-1\rangle
, apply the transformation
|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle\mapsto|\mathrm{good}\rangle
. This can be achieved by applying a Pauli-
X
gate on the second qubit of flag register.

(b)

Undo the branch marking by invoking the reversal of
Proposition
22
.

Let us first confirm that the above is indeed a variable time quantum algorithm in the sense of
Definition
1
. To this end, we define the clock projections

\Pi_{j}=\sum_{0\leq x\leq j-1}|x\rangle\!\langle x|\otimes\left(|\mathrm{good}\rangle\!\langle\mathrm{good}|+|\mathrm{bad}\rangle\!\langle\mathrm{bad}|\right)

(86)

for
j=0,\ldots,m
. Note that
\Pi_{m}=I
holds effectively since the last step of our algorithm always maps
|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle
to
|\mathrm{good}\rangle
. The flag projection is naturally selected to be

\Pi_{b}=|\mathrm{bad}\rangle\!\langle\mathrm{bad}|.

(87)

Finally, we have the following input algorithm at stage
j
(only showing its nontrivial actions):

\displaystyle C_{j}

\displaystyle=|j\rangle\!\langle j-1|\otimes\sum_{u}\xi_{j,u,1}|\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}\rangle\!\langle\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}|\otimes|\pm,\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\!\langle\pm,\phi_{u,Y\pm}|

(88)

\displaystyle+|j-1\rangle\!\langle j-1|\otimes\sum_{u}\xi_{j,u,0}\left(\frac{3^{j}}{3^{m}}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\sqrt{1-\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}}|\mathrm{bad}\rangle\right)\langle\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}|\otimes|\pm,\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\!\langle\pm,\phi_{u,Y\pm}|.

It is then a routine verification that
C_{j}\Pi_{j-1}=\Pi_{j-1}
holds for all
j=1,\ldots,m-1
. This is also true for
j=m
if we define
C_{m}=|m-1\rangle\!\langle m-1|\otimes|\mathrm{good}\rangle\!\langle\mathrm{cont^{\prime}d}|
(corresponding to setting
\xi_{m,u,0}=1
for all
u
). For notational convenience, we may assume that branch marking and its inverse are incorporated into
C_{1}
and
C_{m}
, respectively.

To simplify the discussion, we consider the case where the branch marking can be performed perfectly, corresponding to input algorithms
B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}
. This produces the output state

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\zeta_{j,u}|j-1\rangle\left(\frac{3^{j}}{3^{m}}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\sqrt{1-\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}}|\mathrm{bad}\rangle\right)\gamma_{u}\left(|+,\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle+|-,\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle\right),

(89)

where
\zeta_{j,u}
are the cumulative coefficients at stages
j

\zeta_{j,u}=\xi_{j,u,0}\prod_{l=1}^{j-1}\xi_{l,u,1}.

(90)

Moreover, we know that GPE has the action:

(i)

For
\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\left[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\right)\subseteq\left[\frac{1}{3^{l}},1\right)
, or equivalently,
l\geq k+1
, it holds
\xi_{l,u,1}\approx 0
and
\xi_{l,u,0}\approx\pm 1
.

(ii)

For
\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\left[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\right)=\left[\frac{1}{3^{l+1}},\frac{1}{3^{l}}\right)
, or equivalently,
l=k
, it holds
\left\lvert\xi_{l,u,0}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\xi_{l,u,1}\right\rvert^{2}=1
.

(iii)

For
\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\left[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\right)\subseteq\left[0,\frac{1}{3^{l+1}}\right)
, or equivalently,
l\leq k-1
, it holds
\xi_{l,u,0}\approx 0
and
\xi_{l,u,1}\approx i
.

This means that the cumulative coefficients satisfy
\zeta_{j,u}\approx 0
if
j\geq k+2
or
j\leq k-1
.
So if we further assume that GPE can be performed perfectly in the passband and stopband, corresponding to input algorithms
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
, then we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\Bigg(\zeta_{k+1,u}|k\rangle\left(\frac{3^{k+1}}{3^{m}}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\sqrt{1-\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}}|\mathrm{bad}\rangle\right)

(94)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{k,u}|k-1\rangle\left(\frac{3^{k}}{3^{m}}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\sqrt{1-\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}}|\mathrm{bad}\rangle\right)\Bigg)\gamma_{u}\left(|+,\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle+|-,\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle\right).

Compared with
Eq.

81
, our
Eq.

94
maintains the uniform superposition of eigenstates
|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle
of the quantum walk operator. At the end, the branch marking register is uncomputed, so the two branches can be merged back to recover the original eigenstate
|\phi_{u}\rangle
of the input matrix.
In the next subsection, we will describe a deterministic amplification schedule for Tunable VTAA to amplify such a state.

4.2
Deterministic amplification schedule

We now describe a deterministic amplification schedule for Tunable VTAA with input algorithms
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
, to prepare a state proportional to

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{d-inv}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left(\zeta_{k+1,u}\frac{3^{k+1}}{3^{m}}|k\rangle+\zeta_{k,u}\frac{3^{k}}{3^{m}}|k-1\rangle\right)\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,

(95)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left(\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}+\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\right)=p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}.

Note however that in defining the above state, we have implicitly assumed that GPE and branch marking can both be performed with zero error. If branch marking is perfect but GPE is erroneous, the corresponding input algorithms are denoted by
B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}
and the resulting amplified state is

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{d-inv},m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\zeta_{j,u}\frac{3^{j}}{3^{m}}|j-1\rangle\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv},m}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}=p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}.

Finally, in the most general case where branch marking and GPE are both erroneous, we denote the input algorithms by
C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}
and the amplified output state by
\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}
, with probability
\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}\right\rVert^{2}=p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}
.

For presentational purposes, most of our analysis in this subsection assumes that GPE and branch marking are both perfect, corresponding to the input algorithms
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
, and we know the precise value of
p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}=\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv}}\right\rVert^{2}
. This is without loss of generality, because the asymptotic scaling of VTAA is not affected if a constant multiplicative approximation of the success probability is used instead.
Moreover, we can set the accuracy
\epsilon_{\text{bm}}
and
\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}
sufficiently small so that

p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}\right)=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\right)=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ}}\right);

a similar relation holds for the amplification thresholds. See
Appendix
E
(in particular
Proposition
25
and
Proposition
26
) for a detailed explanation.

Following the discussion in
Section
1.4
, we consider the amplification thresholds

\alpha_{j}=\begin{cases}c^{2}9^{j-m+l}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2},\quad&j=m-l+1,\ldots,m,\\
0,&j=1,\ldots,m-l,\end{cases}

(96)

for some constant
c\gtrapprox 1
(say
c=1.001
) and integer
0\leq l\leq m
to be determined later.

Analysis of amplification threshold

From
Proposition
4
, we should aim for
\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\alpha_{j}=\mathbf{O}(1)
to avoid a large loss factor (which would otherwise cause a waste of oracular queries). Here, we can use
Eq.

42
and
Eq.

94
to evaluate the potentially good probabilities as

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}

(97)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{m}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=j}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{k=j+1}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}+\sum_{k=0}^{j}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}.

Using this, we compute the sum of threshold values:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\alpha_{j}=\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}c^{2}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

(98)

\displaystyle\leq c^{2}\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\Bigg(\sum_{k=j}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=j+1}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{j}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\Bigg)9^{j-m+l}

\displaystyle=c^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{l}\Bigg(\sum_{k=j+m-l}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{j+m-l-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=j+m-l+1}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{j+m-l}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\Bigg)9^{j}.

By exchanging the order of summation, we may bound first line of the result as

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{l}\bigg(\sum_{k=j+m-l}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{j+m-l-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}\bigg)9^{j}

(99)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=m-l+1}^{m-1}\sum_{j=1}^{k-m+l}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}9^{j}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{j=k-m+l+1}^{l}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}9^{j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9}{8}\sum_{k=m-l+1}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}9^{k-m+l}+\frac{9}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}9^{l}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{4}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}9^{l}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{l}\bigg(\sum_{k=j+m-l+1}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{j+m-l}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\bigg)9^{j}

(100)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=m-l+2}^{m-1}\sum_{j=1}^{k-m+l-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}9^{j}+\sum_{k=0}^{m}\sum_{j=k-m+l}^{l}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}9^{j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9}{8}\sum_{k=m-l+2}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}9^{k-m+l-1}+\frac{9}{8}\sum_{k=0}^{m}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}9^{l}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{4}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}9^{l}.

Altogether, we obtain

\displaystyle c^{2}p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}9^{l}=\alpha_{m}\leq\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\alpha_{j}=\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}c^{2}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

(101)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5c^{2}}{4}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\right)9^{l}

\displaystyle=\frac{5c^{2}}{4}p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}9^{l}.

We thus have
\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\alpha_{j}\leq 1
as long as

l\leq\mathbf{Floor}\left(\log_{3}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\right)\right)=\mathbf{O}\left(\log_{3}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\right)
.
In particular, there is no over amplification and the total loss factor of VTAA is
\geq\frac{5}{6}
.

Analysis of amplification schedule

Next, let us derive the deterministic amplification schedule. At the beginning,
j=m-l+1
, and we compare

\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m-l+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{m-l+1}\widetilde{A}_{m-l}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert=\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m-l+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{m-l+1}A_{m-l}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

(102)

and

\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{\alpha_{m-l+1}}=c\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m-l+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{m-l+1}A_{m-l}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert.

(103)

Assuming
c\gtrapprox 1
(say
c=1.001
), we find that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m-l+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{m-l+1}\widetilde{A}_{m-l}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{\alpha_{m-l+1}},

(104)

\displaystyle 3\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m-l+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{m-l+1}\widetilde{A}_{m-l}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{\alpha_{m-l+1}},

so
3
amplification steps are needed for
j=m-l+1
. After that,

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{6}9\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m-l+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{m-l+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}\right)3\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m-l+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{m-l+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

(105)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m-l+1}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{m-l+1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 3\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m-l+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{m-l+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert.

By induction, suppose we have

\displaystyle\prod_{k=m-l+1}^{j}\left(1-\frac{1}{6}9^{k-m+l}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}\right)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert 3^{j-m+l}

(106)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert 3^{j-m+l}

after stage
j
. For
j+1
, we want to compare

\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

(107)

and

\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{\alpha_{j+1}}=c3^{j-m+l}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert.

(108)

Using the inductive hypothesis and
Eq.

44
,

\displaystyle\prod_{k=m-l+1}^{j}\left(1-\frac{1}{6}9^{k-m+l}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}\right)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert 3^{j-m+l}

(109)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert 3^{j-m+l}.

Then since the loss factor
\geq\left(\frac{5}{6}\right)^{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\alpha_{j}}\geq\frac{5}{6}
, the amplitude satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle<\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{\alpha_{j+1}},

(110)

\displaystyle 3\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{\alpha_{j+1}}.

So again we need
3
amplification steps for
j+1
. After that,

\displaystyle\prod_{k=m-l+1}^{j+1}\left(1-\frac{1}{6}9^{k-m+l}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{k}\Pi_{b}}A_{k}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}\right)\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert 3^{j+1-m+l}

(111)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{6}9\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}\right)3\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{j+1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 3\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert 3^{j+1-m+l}.

Here, the first inequality follows from the inductive hypothesis and the calculation

\displaystyle 9\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=9\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}}

\displaystyle=9\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}\frac{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{j}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}}{\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 9\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j+1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l},

where we have used
Eq.

44
in the second equality and
Lemma
5
in the last inequality (applied from the first nontrivial stage
s_{1}=m-l+1
up to stage
s_{j-m+l}=j
with the amplitudes corresponding to
s_{0}=0
canceled out).
The induction is now complete. We obtain the following deterministic amplification schedule as desired:

2r_{j}+1=\begin{cases}3,\quad&j=m-l+1,\ldots,m,\\
1,&j=1,\ldots,m-l.\end{cases}

(112)

Analysis of query complexity

We now consider the query complexity. Invoking
Proposition
6
,
Proposition
22
and
Proposition
23
as well as the choice of thresholds in
Eq.

96
, we have the cost

\displaystyle\mathbf{O}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{m-l+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right)

(113)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\sum_{j=m-l+2}^{m}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{j-1}}}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j-1}\Pi_{b}}A_{j-1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{j}\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{O}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{b}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{bm}}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{A}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\sum_{j=1}^{m-l+1}3^{j}\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{A})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\sum_{j=m-l+2}^{m}3^{m-l-j}3^{j}\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{A}\right)\Bigg),

where we have again used
p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ}}\right)
.
Here, the cost of GPE grows exponentially with the stage number
j
like
\sim 3^{j}
, suggesting a non-uniform error distribution of the form

\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}=\begin{cases}\frac{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}{l},\quad&j=m-l+2,\ldots,m,\\
\frac{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}{l\cdot 2^{m-l+2-j}},&j=1,\ldots,m-l+1,\end{cases}\qquad\qquad\sum_{j=1}^{m}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}\leq\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}.

(114)

This then leads to the cost

\displaystyle\mathbf{O}\Bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{b}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{bm}}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{A}\right)

(115)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\sum_{j=1}^{m-l+1}3^{j}(m-l+2-j)\log\left(\frac{l}{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{A})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\sum_{j=m-l+2}^{m}3^{m-l-j}3^{j}\log\left(\frac{l}{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{A}\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{b}\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{\text{bm}}}\right)+\frac{l3^{m}}{3^{l}}\log\left(\frac{l}{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}\right)\right)\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{A}\right)\right),

which decreases as
l
increases. So we should choose the largest possible
l
:

l=\mathbf{Floor}\left(\log_{3}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\right)\right)=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(\log_{3}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\right).

(116)

Analysis of success probability

As an immediate consequence of the deterministic schedule, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\geq\frac{5}{6}3^{l}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}A_{m}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

(117)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{5}{6}3^{\log_{3}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\right)-1}\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{5}{6}\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}=\frac{\sqrt{5}}{9c},

where the first inequality follows from
Lemma
5
(applied from the first nontrivial stage
s_{1}=m-l+1
up to stage
s_{l}=m
).
So VTAA outputs the normalized version of discretized inverse state
\psi_{\text{d-inv}}
with a constant success probability.

Analysis of error

With this deterministic schedule, the initial state
|b\rangle
and input algorithms
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m-l+1}
are invoked
3^{l}
times. After that, algorithm
A_{j}
is invoked
3^{m-j+1}
times for
j=m-l+2,\ldots,m
. So the total error of preparing the discretized inverse state is at most

3^{l}\epsilon_{\text{bm}}+3^{l}\sum_{j=1}^{m-l+1}\frac{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}{l\cdot 2^{m-l+2-j}}+\sum_{j=m-l+2}^{m}3^{m-j+1}\frac{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}{l}=\mathbf{O}\left(3^{l}\left(\epsilon_{\text{bm}}+\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}\right)\right)=\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{\text{bm}}+\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right).

(118)

Thus, by setting

\epsilon_{\text{bm}},\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}\epsilon\right),

(119)

we can generate the discretized inverse state
\psi_{\text{d-inv}}
with constant probability, accuracy
\epsilon
and query complexity

\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{b}\right)+\kappa\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}\epsilon}\right)\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{A}\right)\right).

(120)

In the next subsection, we show how to project the error into potentially good subspaces to further improve the query complexity to

\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{b}\right)+\kappa\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{A}\right)\right).

(121)

4.3
Projecting error toward potentially good subspaces

In this subection, we present an improved error analysis for preparing the normalized discretized inverse state by projecting error onto potentially good subspaces.

To begin with, recall that if branch marking were perfectly performed, our discretized inverse state would take the form

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\zeta_{j,u}|j-1\rangle\left(\frac{3^{j}}{3^{m}}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\sqrt{1-\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}}|\mathrm{bad}\rangle\right)\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,

(122)

where
\zeta_{j,u}=\xi_{j,u,0}\prod_{l=1}^{j-1}\xi_{l,u,1}
are the cumulative coefficients from erroneous GPEs, such that the normalization condition
\left\lvert\xi_{l,u,0}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\xi_{l,u,1}\right\rvert^{2}=1
holds at all stages
l
. If GPEs were also performed perfectly, then majority of
\zeta_{j,u}
would be zero unless
j=k,k+1
and we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\Bigg(\zeta_{k+1,u}|k\rangle\left(\frac{3^{k+1}}{3^{m}}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\sqrt{1-\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}}|\mathrm{bad}\rangle\right)

(123)

\displaystyle+\zeta_{k,u}|k-1\rangle\left(\frac{3^{k}}{3^{m}}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\sqrt{1-\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}}|\mathrm{bad}\rangle\right)\Bigg)\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle.

Let us compare these two states, but only within the potentially good subspaces. That is, we compare

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{d-inv},m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\zeta_{j,u}|j-1\rangle\frac{3^{j}}{3^{m}}\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,\qquad\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv},m}\right\rVert^{2}=p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m},

(124)

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{d-inv}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left(\zeta_{k+1,u}|k\rangle\frac{3^{k+1}}{3^{m}}+\zeta_{k,u}|k-1\rangle\frac{3^{k}}{3^{m}}\right)\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,\qquad\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv}}\right\rVert^{2}=p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}.

Then their squared Euclidean distance is bounded by

\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv},m}-\psi_{\text{d-inv}}\right\rVert^{2}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j\neq k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}.

(125)

We divide the analysis into two cases.

(i)

j\leq k-1
: In this case, we use the bound

\begin{cases}\left\lvert\xi_{l,u,1}\right\rvert\leq 1,\quad&l=1,\ldots,j-1,\\
\left\lvert\xi_{j,u,0}\right\rvert\leq\epsilon_{\text{qpe},j}.&\end{cases}

(126)

to obtain

\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert\leq\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}.

(127)

(ii)

j\geq k+2
: In this case, we use the bound

\begin{cases}\left\lvert\xi_{l,u,1}\right\rvert\leq 1,\quad&l=1,\ldots,k,\\
\left\lvert\xi_{l,u,1}\right\rvert\leq\epsilon_{\text{gpe},l},\quad&l=k+1,\ldots,j-1,\\
\left\lvert\xi_{j,u,0}\right\rvert\leq 1,\end{cases}

(128)

to obtain

\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert\leq\prod_{l=k+1}^{j-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},l}.

(129)

This bounds the squared distance by

\displaystyle\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv},m}-\psi_{\text{d-inv}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}+\sum_{j=k+2}^{m}\prod_{l=k+1}^{j-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},l}^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}\right)

(130)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}^{2}\frac{9^{k-1}}{9^{m}}+\sum_{j=k+2}^{m}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j-1}^{2}\frac{9^{k+2}}{9^{m}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+2}}{9^{m}}\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}\right)^{2}\leq 9p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2},

where

p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}.

(131)

Here, we assume all
\epsilon_{\text{gpe},l}\leq\frac{1}{3}
in the second inequality and upper bound the
\ell_{2}
-norm by
\ell_{1}
-norm in the third inequality.

Now we take the error of branch marking into account. If the actual output state is
\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}
with
\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}\right\rVert^{2}=p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}
, then

\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}-\psi_{\text{d-inv},m}\right\rVert\leq 2\epsilon_{\text{bm}}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}-\psi_{\text{d-inv}}\right\rVert=\mathbf{O}\left(\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}}\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}+\epsilon_{\text{bm}}\right).

(132)

We will prove in
Proposition
25
from
Appendix
E
that

p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}\right)=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\right)=\mathbf{\Theta}(p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1})=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ}}\right).

(133)

Hence, by
[
40
, Lemma 24]
,

\left\lVert\frac{\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}}}-\frac{\psi_{\text{d-inv}}}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\right\rVert\leq\frac{2\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}-\psi_{\text{d-inv}}\right\rVert}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}=\mathbf{O}\left(\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}+\frac{\epsilon_{\text{bm}}}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right),

(134)

We see that the error of GPE shrinks by a factor of
\mathbf{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}\right)
when projected onto the potentially good subspaces, which cancels with the state normalization factor
\mathbf{\Theta}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)
. Hence, we choose

\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}=\mathbf{\Theta}(\epsilon),\qquad\epsilon_{\text{bm}}=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}\epsilon\right),

(135)

obtaining the claimed query complexity of preparing discretized inverse state

\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{b}\right)+\kappa\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{A}\right)\right).

(136)

4.4
Solution norm estimation

Up to this point, we have assumed that a constant multiplicative estimate of the solution norm
\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert
is available a prior. This assumption is equivalent to knowing
p_{\text{succ}}
and
p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}
to a constant multiplicative accuracy, which is necessary to achieve the
\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)
scaling for initial state preparation. In this subsection, we show that this strictly linear scaling can be attained even with an unknown solution norm, when
p_{\text{succ}}
is replaced by its lower bound
\alpha_{p_{\text{succ}}}\leq p_{\text{succ}}
. In fact, we will describe a solution norm estimation algorithm whose query complexity of the initial state oracle has the scaling
\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{p_{\text{succ}}}}}\right)
.

We begin by introducing the amplitude estimation algorithm.

Lemma 7

(Amplitude estimation)
.

Let
|\psi_{0}\rangle
be a quantum state and
\Pi
be an orthogonal projection. Then for any
\epsilon,\delta>0
, there exists a quantum algorithm that outputs
y
with

\mathbf{P}\left(\left\lvert y-\left\lVert\Pi|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\right\rvert\geq\epsilon\right)<\delta

(137)

using

\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right)

(138)

queries to controlled-preparation of
|\psi_{0}\rangle
, controlled-reflection
I-2\Pi
and their inverses.

Note that in applications where the target accuracy
\epsilon=\mathbf{\Theta}(1)
is constant, it is possible to perform amplitude estimation by repeatedly measuring outcome of the algorithm and applying Chernoff bound. Otherwise if a smaller
\epsilon
is desired, one can use quantum phase estimation
[
11
]
.

To simplify the discussion, we first consider the discretized inverse state with an unknown
p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}=\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv}}\right\rVert^{2}
but a known lower bound
\alpha_{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}
. We then (mathematically) define

l^{*}=\mathbf{Floor}\left(\log_{3}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\right)\right).

(139)

If we run Tunable VTAA with
l^{*}
, our analysis in
Section
4.2
then shows that the success amplitude is at least

\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\geq\frac{\sqrt{5}}{9c}=\frac{5\sqrt{5}}{45c}.

(140)

Our claim is that if
l<l^{*}
is sufficiently small, the success amplitude is constant gapped below
\frac{\sqrt{5}}{9c}
. Indeed, if
l\leq l^{*}-2
, we invoke
Lemma
5
to get

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}\widetilde{A}_{m}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 3^{l}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{m}\Pi_{b}}A_{m}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert\leq 3^{l^{*}-2}\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}

(141)

\displaystyle\leq 3^{\log_{3}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\right)-2}\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\frac{1}{9}\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}=\frac{2\sqrt{5}}{45c}.

Our solution norm estimation algorithm proceeds as follows. We run Tunable VTAA with pre-merging parameter
l=0,1,2,\ldots
, accuracy
\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}=\mathbf{\Theta}(1)
and
\epsilon_{\text{bm}}=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}\right)
, and we perform amplitude estimation with a sufficiently small (yet constant) accuracy and failure probability
\delta_{l}
.
When iterating through
l=0,1,2,\ldots
, with high probability we see the estimated amplitude below
\frac{\sqrt{5}}{15c}=\frac{3\sqrt{5}}{45c}
for all
l=0,1,\ldots,l^{*}-2
and amplitude above
\frac{4\sqrt{5}}{45c}
for either
l=l^{*}-1,l^{*}
.
Supposing the first time we see the amplitude above
\frac{4\sqrt{5}}{45c}
is at some
l
, we consider the value
l+1
. Then, we have

\log_{3}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\right)-1\leq l^{*}\leq l+1\leq l^{*}+1\leq\log_{3}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\right)+1,

(142)

which implies

\frac{1}{3}\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}\leq 3^{l+1}\leq 3\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}.

(143)

Hence,
\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}3^{l+1}c}
is a
3
-multiplicative approximation of
\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}
.

Note that for each fixed
l
, Tunable VTAA has query complexity

\displaystyle 3^{l}\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{m-l+1}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right)+\sum_{j=m-l+2}^{m}3^{m-j+1}\mathbf{Cost}\left(A_{j}\right)

(144)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{O}\left(3^{l}\mathbf{Cost}(O_{b})+3^{l}\sum_{j=1}^{m-l+1}3^{j}\log\left(2^{m-l+2-j}l\right)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{A})+\sum_{j=m-l+2}^{m}3^{m-j+1}3^{j}\log(l)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{A})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{O}\left(3^{l}\mathbf{Cost}(O_{b})+3^{m}l\log(l)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{A})\right).

Iterating over
l
and incorporating the cost of amplitude estimation, the total query complexity is thus bounded by

\mathbf{O}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{l^{*}}3^{l}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{l}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{b})+\sum_{l=1}^{l^{*}}3^{m}l\log(l)\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{l}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{A})\right).

(145)

Let us choose the schedule of failure probabilities to be

\delta_{l}=\frac{1}{(\alpha_{l^{*}}-l+3)^{2}},\qquad\alpha_{l^{*}}=\mathbf{Floor}\left(\log_{3}\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}c\sqrt{\alpha_{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}}\right)\right)\geq l^{*}.

(146)

By the union bound, the total failure probability is at most

\sum_{l=1}^{l^{*}}\frac{1}{(\alpha_{l^{*}}-l+3)^{2}}\leq\sum_{l=1}^{\alpha_{l^{*}}}\frac{1}{(\alpha_{l^{*}}-l+3)^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{3^{2}}+\frac{1}{4^{2}}+\cdots=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}-\frac{5}{4}\approx 0.4.

(147)

The query complexity then becomes

\displaystyle\mathbf{O}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{l^{*}}3^{l}\log\left(\alpha_{l^{*}}-l+3\right)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{b})+\kappa\sum_{l=1}^{l^{*}}l\log(l)\log\left(\alpha_{l^{*}}-l+3\right)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{A})\right)

(148)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}}\mathbf{Cost}(O_{b})+\kappa\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}}\right)\log\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{A})\right).

The above analysis handles the discretized inverse state
\psi_{\text{d-inv}}
, and assumes that a lower bound
\alpha_{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}=\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv}}\right\rVert^{2}
is known a prior.
In practice, the output state is instead
\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}
with
\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}\right\rVert^{2}=p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}
, but we would still proceed as above, except we replace all
p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}
by
p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}
, and analyze VTAA using
Proposition
25
and
Proposition
26
.
As per
Proposition
25
, we can also convert the given lower bound
\alpha_{p_{\text{succ}}}\leq p_{\text{succ}}
into some
\alpha_{p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}
.
Consequently, what we actually obtain is a constant multiplicative approximation of
\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}}
. Invoking
Proposition
25
once more, we obtain a constant multiplicative approximation of
\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}
.

Theorem 1

(Solution norm estimation with optimal initial state preparation)
.

Let
A
be the coefficient matrix such that
A/\alpha_{A}
is block encoded by
O_{A}
with normalization factor
\alpha_{A}\geq\left\lVert A\right\rVert
. Let
|b\rangle
be the initial state prepared by oracle
O_{b}
.
Then the solution norm
\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert
, and hence the success amplitude
\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}=\frac{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}}
, can be estimated to a constant multiplicative accuracy and success probability
>\frac{1}{2}
with query complexity

\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{p_{\text{succ}}}}}\mathbf{Cost}(O_{b})+\kappa\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{p_{\text{succ}}}}}\right)\log\log^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha_{p_{\text{succ}}}}}\right)\mathbf{Cost}(O_{A})\right),

(149)

where
\alpha_{A^{-1}}\geq\left\lVert A^{-1}\right\rVert
is a norm upper bound on the inverse matrix,
\kappa=\alpha_{A}\alpha_{A^{-1}}
is an upper bound on the spectral condition number, and
\alpha_{p_{\text{succ}}}\leq p_{\text{succ}}
is a lower bound on the success probability.

The algorithm uses a nested amplitude amplification with deterministic schedule
Eq.

112
and an increasing choice of pre-merging parameter
l=0,1,2,\ldots

5
Solving linear systems

We now consider the quantum linear system problem. In
Section
5.1
, we show that the problem can be solved by inverting the input matrix over the discretized inverse state. Combining with the tunable VTAA algorithm from the previous section, we provide a simplified quantum linear system algorithm with optimal queries to the initial state preparation and nearly optimal queries to the coefficient matrix block encoding. We provide a proof of the optimality in
Section
5.2
.

5.1
Matrix inversion over discretized inverse state

Recall from
Section
4
that we can use Tunable VTAA to prepare the following state with accuracy
\epsilon
and constant success probability

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left(\zeta_{k+1,u}\frac{3^{k+1}}{3^{m}}|k\rangle+\zeta_{k,u}\frac{3^{k}}{3^{m}}|k-1\rangle\right)\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,

(150)

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left(\left\lvert\zeta_{k+1,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}+\left\lvert\zeta_{k,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\right).

This state differs from the solution state in that the reciprocal of eigenvalues are replaced by the discrete values
\frac{3^{k}}{3^{m}}
. To get the actual solution state, we perform a block-encoded matrix inversion using the following gadget.

Lemma 8

(Block encoding inversion
[
23
, Corollary 69]
)
.

Let
A
be a matrix such that
A/\alpha_{A}
is block encoded by
O_{A}
with some normalization factor
\alpha_{A}\geq\left\lVert A\right\rVert
.
Then the operator

\frac{A^{-1}}{2\alpha_{A^{-1}}}

(151)

can be block encoded with accuracy
\epsilon
using

\mathbf{O}\left(\kappa\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)\right)

(152)

queries to the controlled-
O_{A}
and its inverse, where
\alpha_{A^{-1}}\geq\left\lVert A^{-1}\right\rVert
is a norm upper bound on the inverse matrix, and
\kappa=\alpha_{A}\alpha_{A^{-1}}
is an upper bound on the spectral condition number.

In our case, we apply the block-encoded inversion of the input matrix with accuracy
\epsilon_{\text{blk}}
, controlled by the clock register:

\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}|k\rangle\!\langle k|\otimes\frac{\Pi_{[\frac{1}{3^{k+2}},1)}A^{-1}\Pi_{[\frac{1}{3^{k+2}},1)}}{2\cdot\frac{3^{k+2}}{\alpha_{A}}},\qquad\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}=\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\mathcal{S}}|\phi_{u}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u}|.

(153)

Here, the clock state
|k\rangle
corresponds to eigenvalues of the input matrix within
\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in[\frac{1}{3^{k+2}},\frac{1}{3^{k}})
. Thus to fully cover all possible eigenvalues, we perform the block-encoded matrix inversion over
[\frac{1}{3^{k+2}},1)
.
This block encoding can be implemented with query complexity
\mathbf{O}\left(\kappa\log(1/\epsilon_{\text{blk}})\right)
, by using the
|k\rangle
register to control the rotations in the quantum signal processing circuit
[
38
]
, and the entire operator has accuracy
\epsilon_{\text{blk}}
due to its block diagonal structure.
With a high probability, we then transform the state into

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,blk}}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left(\zeta_{k+1,u}\frac{1}{3^{m}}\frac{\alpha_{A}}{2\cdot 3}\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}}|k\rangle+\zeta_{k,u}\frac{1}{3^{m}}\frac{\alpha_{A}}{2\cdot 3}\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}}|k-1\rangle\right)\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,

(154)

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,blk}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\frac{1}{3^{m}}\frac{\alpha_{A}}{2\cdot 3}\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}}\right\rvert^{2}=\frac{p_{\text{succ}}}{36}.

This state can be produced with probability close to unity by another amplitude amplification.
Finally, we uncompute the clock register by reversing the GPE (without performing rotations
|\mathrm{good}\rangle\mapsto\frac{3^{j}}{3^{m}}|\mathrm{good}\rangle+\sqrt{1-\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}}|\mathrm{bad}\rangle
or VTAA), obtaining the normalized solution state

\frac{\sum_{u}\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}}\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{\sum_{u}\left\lvert\frac{\gamma_{u}}{\lambda_{u}}\right\rvert^{2}}}=\frac{A^{-1}|b\rangle}{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert}.

(155)

As per
Proposition
25
,

p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}\right)=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\right)=\mathbf{\Theta}(p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1})=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ}}\right)=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ,blk}}\right),

so we only need at most constant rounds of amplitude amplifications beyond the preparation of discretized inverse state by Tunable VTAA. Setting
\epsilon_{\text{blk}}=\mathbf{\Theta}(\epsilon)
, we have thus established the main algorithm.

Theorem 2

(Quantum linear system algorithm with optimal initial state preparation)
.

Let
A
be the coefficient matrix such that
A/\alpha_{A}
is block encoded by
O_{A}
with normalization factor
\alpha_{A}\geq\left\lVert A\right\rVert
. Let
|b\rangle
be the initial state prepared by oracle
O_{b}
.
Suppose that the solution norm
\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert
, and hence the success amplitude
\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}=\frac{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}}
, can be estimated to a constant multiplicative accuracy.
Then the quantum state

\frac{A^{-1}|b\rangle}{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert}

(156)

can be prepared to accuracy
\epsilon
and success probability
>\frac{1}{2}
with query complexity

\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{b}\right)+\kappa\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\log\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)}{\epsilon}\right)\mathbf{Cost}\left(O_{A}\right)\right),

(157)

where
\alpha_{A^{-1}}\geq\left\lVert A^{-1}\right\rVert
is a norm upper bound on the inverse matrix, and

\kappa=\alpha_{A}\alpha_{A^{-1}}
is an upper bound on the spectral condition number.

The algorithm uses a nested amplitude amplification with deterministic schedule
Eq.

112
and pre-merging parameter
l=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(\log_{3}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p_{\text{succ}}}}\right)\right)
.

5.2
Lower bound

Now, we prove that our query complexity to the initial state preparation is optimal, by showing how the quantum search problem
[
25
]
can be solved by a linear system algorithm. We note that a similar lower bound has been previously derived in
[
52
]
.

Specifically, consider the
d
-dimensional initial state

|b\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\left(\sum_{j\neq w}|j\rangle-|w\rangle\right),

(158)

where
|w\rangle\in\{|0\rangle,|1\rangle,\ldots,|d-1\rangle\}
is an arbitrary unknown basis state. This state can be (and only be) prepared by making
1
query to the Grover oracle with the uniform superposition state. We then perform an orthogonal decomposition of
|b\rangle
along the uniform superposition state to get

|b\rangle=\frac{d-2}{d}|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle+\frac{2\sqrt{d-1}}{d}|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle,

(159)

where using the notation of
Appendix
C.3

|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{j}|j\rangle,\qquad|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle=\sum_{j\neq w}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(d-1)d}}|j\rangle+\sqrt{\frac{d-1}{d}}|w\rangle.

(160)

Now, we define the coefficient matrix

A=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\left(I-|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{ZX+}|\right)+|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{ZX+}|.

(161)

Note that
A
can be block encoded with normalization factor
\alpha_{A}=1
and zero query to the Grover oracle. Then the inverse matrix

A^{-1}=\sqrt{d}\left(I-|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{ZX+}|\right)+|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{ZX+}|

(162)

has spectral norm

\left\lVert A^{-1}\right\rVert=\sqrt{d}=\alpha_{A^{-1}}.

(163)

We now invoke the quantum linear system algorithm, obtaining a state within Euclidean distance
\epsilon_{\text{lin}}
to the solution
\frac{A^{-1}|b\rangle}{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert}
.
Here,

\displaystyle A^{-1}|b\rangle

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\frac{d-1}{d}}|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle+\frac{d-2}{d}|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle

(165)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\neq w}\frac{d-2+2\sqrt{d}}{d\sqrt{d}}|j\rangle-\frac{2(d-1)\sqrt{d}-d+2}{d\sqrt{d}}|w\rangle,

which implies

\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert=\sqrt{\frac{5d^{2}-8d+4}{d^{2}}}.

(166)

So the inverse success amplitude is

\frac{\left\lVert A^{-1}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert}=\sqrt{d}\sqrt{\frac{d^{2}}{5d^{2}-8d+4}}=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{d}\right).

(167)

Meanwhile, the absolute value of amplitude of the desired
|w\rangle
is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\langle w|A^{-1}|b\rangle}{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert}\right\rvert-\epsilon_{\text{lin}}

\displaystyle\geq\left\lvert\frac{\frac{2(d-1)\sqrt{d}+d-2}{d\sqrt{d}}}{\sqrt{\frac{5d^{2}-8d+4}{d^{2}}}}\right\rvert-\epsilon_{\text{lin}}\geq\frac{\frac{2(d-1)\sqrt{d}-2}{d\sqrt{d}}}{\sqrt{\frac{5d^{2}+4}{d^{2}}}}-\epsilon_{\text{lin}}

(168)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\frac{2(d-2)\sqrt{d}}{d\sqrt{d}}}{\sqrt{\frac{5(d+1)^{2}}{d^{2}}}}-\epsilon_{\text{lin}}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}\frac{d-2}{d+1}-\epsilon_{\text{lin}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}\left(1-\frac{3}{d+1}\right)-\epsilon_{\text{lin}}\geq\frac{13}{8\sqrt{5}}-\epsilon_{\text{lin}}

when
d\geq 15
. Let us choose
\epsilon_{\text{lin}}=\frac{1}{60}
,
so the probability of getting outcome
|w\rangle
is at least
\left(\frac{13}{8\sqrt{5}}-\frac{1}{60}\right)^{2}>0.504
.
This thus solves the Grover search problem, and we conclude that the query complexity to the initial state is at least

\mathbf{\Omega}\left(\sqrt{d}\right)=\mathbf{\Omega}\left(\frac{\left\lVert A^{-1}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert}\right).

(171)

Theorem 3

(Lower bound on initial state preparation cost)
.

For any
0<p<1
, there exists a linear system with coefficient matrix
A
and initial state
|b\rangle
(prepared by
O_{b}
) satisfying

\frac{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert^{2}}{\left\lVert A^{-1}\right\rVert^{2}}=\mathbf{\Theta}(p),

(172)

such that

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{g\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\ \Big|\ \exists\text{ a quantum algorithm }V\text{ making }g\text{ queries to }O_{b},

(173)

\displaystyle\left\lVert V|0\rangle-\frac{A^{-1}|b\rangle}{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert}\right\rVert=\mathbf{O}(1)\text{ sufficiently small}\bigg\}=\mathbf{\Omega}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right).

6
Block preconditioning

In this section, we introduce the technique of
block preconditioning
, and describe its algorithmic applications. Specifically, we present a simple quantum linear system solver in
Section
6.1
with optimal queries to the coefficient matrix block encoding by choosing the initial state itself as the preconditioner. Combining block preconditioning with our main algorithms
Theorem
1
and
Theorem
2
, we then show how to reduce the cost of initial state preparation in solving differential equations (
Section
6.2
), estimating real eigenvalues of non-normal matrices (
Section
6.3
), transforming matrices with real spectra and preparing their ground states (
Section
6.4
). Additionally, we apply block preconditioning to improve the block encoded version of quantum eigenvalue transformer in
Section
6.5
.

6.1
Quantum linear system solver

Our quantum linear system algorithm in
Theorem
2
has an optimal query complexity of the initial state preparation, and a nearly optimal cost of the coefficient block encoding. We now show that it is possible to solve a preconditioned quantum linear system problem, producing the same solution state but making an optimal number of queries to the block encoding oracle.

Specifically, we choose the preconditioner to be the initial state
|b\rangle
itself and define the scaling matrix
