\lambda_{u}=\pm 1
,
\left\{G|\phi_{u}\rangle\right\}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{H}_{u}
, under which

U=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{u}\end{bmatrix},\qquad GG^{\dagger}=\begin{bmatrix}1\end{bmatrix}.

(298)

3.

When
-1<\lambda_{u}<1
,
\left\{G|\phi_{u}\rangle,\frac{UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}}\right\}
is an orthonormal basis for
\mathcal{H}_{u}
, under which

U=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{u}&\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}\\
\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}&-\lambda_{u}\end{bmatrix},\qquad GG^{\dagger}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}.

(299)

4.

Restricted to
\mathcal{H}_{\bot}
,
U
is still a unitary and
GG^{\dagger}=0
.

Proof.

We start by checking the pairwise orthogonality of
\mathcal{H}_{u}
and
\mathcal{H}_{v}
for
u\neq v
:

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{v}|G^{\dagger}G|\phi_{u}\rangle=0,\qquad\langle\phi_{v}|G^{\dagger}UG|\phi_{u}\rangle=\lambda_{u}\langle\phi_{v}|\phi_{u}\rangle=0,

(300)

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{v}|G^{\dagger}U^{\dagger}G|\phi_{u}\rangle=\lambda_{v}\langle\phi_{v}|\phi_{u}\rangle=0,\qquad\langle\phi_{v}|G^{\dagger}U^{\dagger}UG|\phi_{u}\rangle=\langle\phi_{v}|\phi_{u}\rangle=0.

Combining with the analysis proceeding this theorem, we have established the claimed orthogonal decomposition. Since
\bigobot_{u}\mathcal{H}_{u}
is invariant under the normal operators
U
,
U^{\dagger}
and
GG^{\dagger}
, its orthogonal complement
\mathcal{H}_{\bot}
is also invariant under
U
,
U^{\dagger}
and
GG^{\dagger}
.

The statement about 1D subspaces is already proved in
Lemma
17
.
When
\dim(\mathcal{H}_{u})=2
, we can construct an orthonormal basis by applying the Gram-Schmidt process to
\left\{G|\phi_{u}\rangle,UG|\phi_{u}\rangle\right\}
. This produces the unit basis vector

\displaystyle\frac{UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-G|\phi_{u}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u}|G^{\dagger}UG|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\left\lVert UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-G|\phi_{u}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u}|G^{\dagger}UG|\phi_{u}\rangle\right\rVert}

\displaystyle=\frac{UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\left\lVert UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle\right\rVert}

(301)

\displaystyle=\frac{UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{u}^{2}-\lambda_{u}\langle\phi_{u}|G^{\dagger}UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}\langle\phi_{u}|G^{\dagger}U^{\dagger}G|\phi_{u}\rangle}}

\displaystyle=\frac{UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}}

orthogonal to
G|\phi_{u}\rangle
. The matrix representation then follows from a direct calculation.
∎

C.2
Quantum walk

For a 2D subspace
\mathcal{H}_{u}
where
-1<\lambda_{u}<1
, we see that

|\phi_{u,0}\rangle=G|\phi_{u}\rangle,\qquad|\phi_{u,1}\rangle=\frac{UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}}

is an orthonormal basis, under which

U=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{u}&\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}\\
\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}&-\lambda_{u}\end{bmatrix}=\lambda_{u}Z+\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}X,\qquad GG^{\dagger}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}I+\frac{1}{2}Z.

(302)

This means that the quantum walk operator defined by
W=\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)U
has the matrix representation

W=\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)U=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{u}&\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}\\
-\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}&\lambda_{u}\end{bmatrix}=\lambda_{u}I+i\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}Y.

(303)

given by a linear combination of Pauli operators.
Therefore, for each
u
,
W
has two eigenvalues

\lambda_{u,\pm}=\lambda_{u}\pm i\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}=e^{\pm i\arccos(\lambda_{u})}

(304)

with the associated eigenvectors

|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle=\frac{|\phi_{u,0}\rangle\pm i|\phi_{u,1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}.

(305)

We thus obtain the following spectral decomposition of the walk operator.

Corollary 19

(Quantum walk)
.

Let
U
be a unitary operator and
G
be an isometry, such that
G^{\dagger}UG
is Hermitian and
G^{\dagger}U^{2}G=I
.
If
G^{\dagger}UG
has the spectral decomposition

G^{\dagger}UG=\sum_{u}\lambda_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u}|,

(306)

then the quantum walk operator
W=\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)U
has the spectral decomposition

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)U

(307)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\left\lvert\lambda_{u}\right\rvert=1}\lambda_{u}|\phi_{u,0}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,0}|+\sum_{\left\lvert\lambda_{u}\right\rvert<1}\left(e^{+i\arccos(\lambda_{u})}|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,+}|+e^{-i\arccos(\lambda_{u})}|\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,-}|\right)

within
\mathbf{Im}\left(GG^{\dagger}\right)+\mathbf{Im}\left(UGG^{\dagger}U^{\dagger}\right)
, where

|\phi_{u,0}\rangle=G|\phi_{u}\rangle,\qquad|\phi_{u,1}\rangle=\frac{UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}},\qquad|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle=\frac{|\phi_{u,0}\rangle\pm i|\phi_{u,1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}.

(308)

A similar analysis applies to
U
. Recall that
U
is represented as a
2
-by-
2
matrix which can be expanded as a linear combination of Pauli operators
Z
and
X
. Through diagonalization, we find that
U
has eigenvectors

|\phi_{u,ZX\pm}\rangle=\frac{\sqrt{1\pm\lambda_{u}}|\phi_{u,0}\rangle\pm\sqrt{1\mp\lambda_{u}}|\phi_{u,1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}

with corresponding eigenvalues
\pm 1
. We summarize the spectral properties of operators
2GG^{\dagger}-I
,
W
and
U
in
Table
4
.

Operator

Matrix representation

Eigenvalues

Eigenvectors

G^{\dagger}UG

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda_{u}\end{smallmatrix}\right]

\lambda_{u}

|\phi_{u}\rangle

2GG^{\dagger}-I

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right]

\pm 1

|\phi_{u,0}\rangle=G|\phi_{u}\rangle,\ |\phi_{u,1}\rangle=\frac{UG|\phi_{u}\rangle-\lambda_{u}G|\phi_{u}\rangle}{\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}}

U

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda_{u}&\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}\\
\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}&-\lambda_{u}\end{smallmatrix}\right]

\pm 1

|\phi_{u,ZX\pm}\rangle=\frac{\sqrt{1\pm\lambda_{u}}|\phi_{u,0}\rangle\pm\sqrt{1\mp\lambda_{u}}|\phi_{u,1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}

\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)U

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda_{u}&\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}\\
-\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}&\lambda_{u}\end{smallmatrix}\right]

e^{\pm i\arccos(\lambda_{u})}

|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle=\frac{|\phi_{u,0}\rangle\pm i|\phi_{u,1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}

U\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)

\left[\begin{smallmatrix}\lambda_{u}&-\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}\\
\sqrt{1-\lambda_{u}^{2}}&\lambda_{u}\end{smallmatrix}\right]

e^{\mp i\arccos(\lambda_{u})}

|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle=\frac{|\phi_{u,0}\rangle\pm i|\phi_{u,1}\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}

Table 4
:
Spectral properties of common operators involved in Hermitian qubitization. Here, the matrix representation of
G^{\dagger}UG
is defined with respect to the basis
\{|\phi_{u}\rangle\}
, while the matrix representation of
2GG^{\dagger}-I
,
\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)U
,
U\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)
, and
U
is defined relative to
\{|\phi_{u,0}\rangle,|\phi_{u,1}\rangle\}
.

C.3
Quantum amplitude amplification

We now give a self-contained description of quantum amplitude amplification based on qubitization.
Let
|\psi_{0}\rangle
be a quantum state and
\Pi
be an orthogonal projection, so that directly measuring
|\psi_{0}\rangle
yields the outcome
\Pi
with success amplitude

\left\lVert\Pi|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert=\sin(\theta).

Here, amplitude amplification is infeasible if
\theta=0
, whereas the amplitude is already maximally amplified when
\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}
. So we assume
0<\theta<\frac{\pi}{2}
in the following analysis.

We let

G=|\psi_{0}\rangle,\qquad U=I-2\Pi,

and verify that
G
is an isometry and
U
is a Hermitian unitary. Then,

G^{\dagger}UG=\langle\psi_{0}|(I-2\Pi)|\psi_{0}\rangle=1-2\left\lVert\Pi|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}=\cos(2\theta)=\lambda,

where we have dropped the subscript
u
since
G^{\dagger}UG
has only one eigenvalue.
This implies

|\phi_{0}\rangle=|\psi_{0}\rangle,\qquad|\phi_{1}\rangle=\frac{-2\Pi|\psi_{0}\rangle+2\sin^{2}(\theta)|\psi_{0}\rangle}{\sin(2\theta)}.

Within the 2D subspace
\mathbf{Span}\left\{|\phi_{0}\rangle,|\phi_{1}\rangle\right\}
,
U
and
2GG^{\dagger}-I
has the matrix representation

U=\begin{bmatrix}\cos(2\theta)&\sin(2\theta)\\
\sin(2\theta)&-\cos(2\theta)\end{bmatrix},\qquad 2GG^{\dagger}-I=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix}.

Hence, for any nonnegative integer
r
,

\left(-(I-2|\psi_{0}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{0}|)(I-2\Pi)\right)^{r}=\left(\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)U\right)^{r}=\begin{bmatrix}\cos(2r\theta)&\sin(2r\theta)\\
-\sin(2r\theta)&\cos(2r\theta)\end{bmatrix}.

Applied to the initial state, this gives the amplified state

\left(-(I-2|\psi_{0}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{0}|)(I-2\Pi)\right)^{r}|\psi_{0}\rangle=\left(\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)U\right)^{r}|\phi_{0}\rangle=\cos(2r\theta)|\phi_{0}\rangle-\sin(2r\theta)|\phi_{1}\rangle.

We now rewrite the initial and amplified states with respect to the eigenvectors
|\phi_{ZX\pm}\rangle
of
U

with eigenvalues
\pm 1
. For the initial state, we have

|\phi_{0}\rangle=\cos(\theta)|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle+\sin(\theta)|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle,

whereas

\displaystyle\left(\left(2GG^{\dagger}-I\right)U\right)^{r}|\phi_{0}\rangle=\cos(2r\theta)|\phi_{0}\rangle-\sin(2r\theta)|\phi_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\cos(2r\theta)\left(\cos(\theta)|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle+\sin(\theta)|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle\right)-\sin(2r\theta)\left(\sin(\theta)|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle-\cos(\theta)|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\cos((2r+1)\theta)|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle+\sin((2r+1)\theta)|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle.

Finally, since the eigenvalues
+1
,
-1
of
U
are mapped to the eigenvalues
0
,
1
of
\Pi=\frac{I-U}{2}
,

\Pi|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle=0,\qquad\Pi|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle=|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle.

This gives the following version of amplitude amplification.

Lemma

(Quantum amplitude amplification)
.

Let
|\psi_{0}\rangle
be a quantum state and
\Pi
be an orthogonal projection. There exist
|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle\in\mathbf{Im}(\Pi)
and
|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle\in\mathbf{Ker}(\Pi)
such that for any nonnegative integer
r=0,1,2,\ldots
,

\displaystyle\left(-(I-2|\psi_{0}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{0}|)(I-2\Pi)\right)^{r}|\psi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sin\left((2r+1)\arcsin(\left\lVert\Pi|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert)\right)|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle+\cos\left((2r+1)\arcsin(\left\lVert\Pi|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert)\right)|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle.

Moreover,

|\phi_{ZX-}\rangle=\frac{\Pi|\psi_{0}\rangle}{\left\lVert\Pi|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert},\qquad|\phi_{ZX+}\rangle=\frac{(I-\Pi)|\psi_{0}\rangle}{\left\lVert(I-\Pi)|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert},

are uniquely determined if
|\psi_{0}\rangle\notin\mathbf{Ker}(\Pi)
and
|\psi_{0}\rangle\notin\mathbf{Im}(\Pi)
respectively.

Appendix D
Gapped phase estimation with branch marking

In this appendix, we provide details on the construction of the gapped phase estimation algorithm with branch marking, which is used in
Section
4.1
for preparing the discretized inverse state.

D.1
Simultaneous Fourier approximation of even and odd functions

We begin by considering the general problem of applying functions to the eigenphases of an input unitary. Specifically, suppose that the given unitary has the spectral decomposition
U=\sum_{v}e^{i\theta_{v}}|\phi_{v}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{v}|
. Our goal is to obtain an operator
V
close to
\sum_{v}\left(f_{a}(\theta_{v})I+if_{c}(\theta_{v})X\right)\otimes|\phi_{v}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{v}|
acting jointly on the input system and an ancilla qubit, for some desired even/odd periodic functions
f_{a}
and
f_{c}
, using oracular queries to the unitary operator
U
and its inverse. This problem is solved by the quantum signal processing technique
[
38
,
41
]
, which we review below.

Lemma 20

(Quantum signal processing)
.

Let
n>0
be an integer,
f_{a}(\theta)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_{j}\cos(k\theta)
and
f_{c}(\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{j}\sin(k\theta)
be real Fourier series, such that
f_{a}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c}^{2}(\theta)\leq 1
for all
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]
and
f_{a}(0)=1
. Given
U
with the spectral decomposition
U=\sum_{v}e^{i\theta_{v}}|\phi_{v}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{v}|
, there exists a quantum circuit
V
acting on the system register and a single-qubit ancilla as

V=\sum_{v}\left(f_{a}(\theta_{v})I+if_{b}(\theta_{v})Z+if_{c}(\theta_{v})X+if_{d}(\theta_{v})Y\right)\otimes|\phi_{v}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{v}|,

(309)

with some even/odd real
2\pi
-periodic functions
f_{b}
/
f_{d}
satisfying
f_{a}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d}^{2}(\theta)=1
for all
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi]
, using at most
2n
queries to
U
and
U^{\dagger}
.

Note that this is slightly different from the setting of QSVT where we are often interested in a single function with a fixed parity—here we want to construct a unitary
V
that simultaneously implements both even and odd functions.
In practice, the desired
f_{a}
and
f_{c}
can be obtained by truncating an infinite Fourier series. We then slightly modify the functions to satisfy the prerequisites
f_{a}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c}^{2}(\theta)\leq 1
and
f_{a}(0)=1
. We will return to this point momentarily.

Now we describe how to construct the Fourier approximation. Our starting point is the following Chebyshev approximation of the sign function
[
37
,
59
]

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\mathbf{T}_{j}(x)\in\begin{cases}[-1,-1+\epsilon],\quad&x\in[-1,-\nu],\\
[-1,1],&x\in[-\nu,\nu],\\
[1-\epsilon,1],&x\in[\nu,1],\end{cases}

(310)

for arbitrary margin
0<\nu<1
, accuracy
\epsilon>0
and some coefficients
\beta_{j}
. Here, one can choose the polynomial degree to scale like

n=\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\nu}\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)\right).

(311)

From this, we then construct a Fourier approximation of the periodic sign function by substituting
x=\sin(\theta)
and
\nu=\sin(\varphi)
, assuming
0<\varphi\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
. Within the period of
[-\pi,\pi]
, it has the following behavior

g(\theta)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\mathbf{T}_{j}(\sin(\theta))\in\begin{cases}[-1,1],\quad&x\in[-\pi,-\pi+\varphi],\\
[-1,-1+\epsilon],&x\in[-\pi+\varphi,-\varphi],\\
[-1,1],&x\in[-\varphi,\varphi],\\
[1-\epsilon,1],&x\in[\varphi,\pi-\varphi],\\
[-1,1],&x\in[\pi-\varphi,\pi].\end{cases}

(312)

Now assuming
0<\varphi\leq\theta_{0}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
, we reflect and shift the periodic sign function to construct the threshold functions for GPE. Specifically, we define

\displaystyle f_{c}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{g(\theta-\theta_{0})-g(-\theta-\theta_{0})}{2}=\frac{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\mathbf{T}_{j}(\sin(\theta-\theta_{0}))-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\mathbf{T}_{j}(\sin(-\theta-\theta_{0}))}{2}

(313)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\sin(j\theta-j\theta_{0})(-1)^{\frac{j-1}{2}}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\sin(-j\theta-j\theta_{0})(-1)^{\frac{j-1}{2}}}{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\sin(j\theta)\cos(j\theta_{0})(-1)^{\frac{j-1}{2}},

and

\displaystyle f_{a}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{-g(\theta-\theta_{0})-g(-\theta-\theta_{0})}{2}=\frac{-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\mathbf{T}_{j}(\sin(\theta-\theta_{0}))-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\mathbf{T}_{j}(\sin(-\theta-\theta_{0}))}{2}

(314)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\sin(j\theta-j\theta_{0})(-1)^{\frac{j-1}{2}}-\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\sin(-j\theta-j\theta_{0})(-1)^{\frac{j-1}{2}}}{2}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ odd}\end{subarray}}^{n}\beta_{j}\cos(j\theta)\sin(j\theta_{0})(-1)^{\frac{j-1}{2}}.

The above functions actually depend on various parameters such as
\theta_{0}
,
\varphi
and
\epsilon
, but when their values are clear from the context, we will suppress these dependences for presentational purposes.
The qualitative behaviors of these functions are shown in
Table
5
and
Figure
3
.

D.2
Branch marking

Recall from
Section
4.1
that our preparation of the discretized inverse state uses the quantum walk operator. When the input matrix has the spectral decomposition

A=\sum_{u}\lambda_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u}|,

(315)

the walk operator has the corresponding spectral decomposition

W=\sum_{u}\left(e^{i\theta_{u,+}}|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y+}|+e^{i\theta_{u,-}}|\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y-}|\right),\qquad\theta_{u,\pm}=\pm\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right).

(316)

Here, each eigenvector
|\phi_{u}\rangle
of the coefficient matrix splits into
2
eigenvectors
|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle
of the quantum walk opearator, and our primary goal is to ensure that these two branches undergo the exact same computation in GPE. This way, they can be merged back to recover
|\phi_{u,0}\rangle
, on which we perform the eigenvalue inversion
\frac{1}{\lambda_{u}}
.

To this end, we choose the block encoding normalization factor
\alpha_{A}\geq 2\left\lVert A\right\rVert
, so that

\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in\left[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\right].

(317)

Then in the quantum walk operator, this eigenvalue corresponds to two eigenphases

\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)\in\left[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}\right],\qquad-\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)\in\left[-\frac{2\pi}{3},-\frac{\pi}{3}\right].

(318)

To distinguish between these cases, we can implement the periodic threshold functions with
\theta_{0}=\varphi=\frac{\pi}{6}
. This gives

f_{c}\left(\pm\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)\right)\approx\pm 1,\qquad f_{a}\left(\pm\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)\right)\approx 0.

(319)

We now carefully analyze the error. Specifically, we consider the two periodic threshold functions
f_{c}(\theta)
and
f_{a}(\theta)
with
\theta_{0}=\varphi=\frac{\pi}{6}
whose actions are given by
Table
5
and
Figure
3
. By symmetry, it suffices to focus on the interval
\left[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}\right]
.
Recall that
f_{c}(\theta)=\frac{g(\theta-\theta_{0})-g(-\theta-\theta_{0})}{2}
and
f_{a}(\theta)=\frac{-g(\theta-\theta_{0})-g(-\theta-\theta_{0})}{2}
, which implies

f_{c}^{2}(\theta)+f_{a}^{2}(\theta)=\frac{g^{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})+g^{2}(-\theta-\theta_{0})}{2}.

(320)

For all
-\pi\leq\theta\leq\pi
, we have from
Table
5
that
\left\lvert g(\theta-\theta_{0})\right\rvert,\left\lvert g(-\theta-\theta_{0})\right\rvert\leq 1
, so
f_{c}^{2}(\theta)+f_{a}^{2}(\theta)\leq 1
always holds. Also we know from
Table
5
that
1-\epsilon\leq f_{a}(0)\leq 1
. Furthermore, for the target interval where
\frac{\pi}{3}\leq\theta\leq\frac{2\pi}{3}
, we have
1-\epsilon\leq g(\theta-\theta_{0})\leq 1
and
-1\leq g(-\theta-\theta_{0})\leq-1+\epsilon
, which implies
1-\epsilon\leq f_{c}(\theta)\leq 1
. To summarize,

\begin{cases}f_{a}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c}^{2}(\theta)\leq 1,\quad&\forall\theta\in[-\pi,\pi],\\
1-\epsilon\leq f_{a}(0)\leq 1,\\
1-\epsilon\leq f_{c}(\theta)\leq 1,&\forall\theta\in\left[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}\right],\\
-1\leq f_{c}(\theta)\leq-1+\epsilon,&\forall\theta\in\left[-\frac{2\pi}{3},-\frac{\pi}{3}\right].\end{cases}

(321)

We now explain how to satisfy the requirements of
Lemma
20
.
We first run the sum-of-squares method
[
41
]
to obtain functions
f_{b,1}(\theta)
, and
f_{d,1}(\theta)
from

f_{a,1}(\theta)=f_{a}(\theta),\qquad f_{c,1}(\theta)=f_{c}(\theta),

(322)

where

\begin{cases}f_{a,1}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,1}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c,1}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d,1}^{2}(\theta)=1,\quad&\forall\theta\in[-\pi,\pi],\\
f_{a,1}^{2}(0)+f_{b,1}^{2}(0)=1.\end{cases}

(323)

Then, we let

f_{a,2}(\theta)=f_{a,1}(\theta)f_{a,1}(0)+f_{b,1}(\theta)f_{b,1}(0),\qquad f_{c,2}(\theta)=f_{c,1}(\theta).

(324)

Note that by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle f_{a,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c,2}^{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\left(f_{a,1}(\theta)f_{a,1}(0)+f_{b,1}(\theta)f_{b,1}(0)\right)^{2}+f_{c,1}^{2}(\theta)

(325)

\displaystyle\leq\left(f_{a,1}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,1}^{2}(\theta)\right)\left(f_{a,1}^{2}(0)+f_{b,1}^{2}(0)\right)+f_{c,1}^{2}(\theta)

\displaystyle=f_{a,1}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,1}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c,1}^{2}(\theta)\leq 1.

Thus, we can rerun the sum-of-squares method to get functions
f_{b,2}(\theta)
, and
f_{d,2}(\theta)
. These functions now satisfy

\begin{cases}f_{a,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d,2}^{2}(\theta)=1,\quad&\forall\theta\in[-\pi,\pi],\\
f_{a,2}(0)=1,\end{cases}

(326)

as desired, resulting in the QSP operator

\sum_{v}\left(f_{a,2}(\theta_{v})I+if_{b,2}(\theta_{v})Z+if_{c,2}(\theta_{v})X+if_{d,2}(\theta_{v})Y\right)\otimes|\phi_{v}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{v}|,

(327)

where
v
goes through all 2-tuple
(u,\pm)
.

It is clear that
f_{c,2}=f_{c,1}=f_{c}
, so we introduce no additional error to the function
f_{c}
:

\begin{cases}1-\epsilon\leq f_{c,2}(\theta)\leq 1,\quad&\forall\theta\in\left[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}\right],\\
-1\leq f_{c,2}(\theta)\leq-1+\epsilon,&\forall\theta\in\left[-\frac{2\pi}{3},-\frac{\pi}{3}\right].\end{cases}

(328)

As for the remaining components,

\displaystyle f_{a,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d,2}^{2}(\theta)=1-f_{c,2}^{2}(\theta)\leq 1-(1-\epsilon)^{2}=2\epsilon-\epsilon^{2},\qquad\forall\theta\in\left[-\frac{2\pi}{3},-\frac{\pi}{3}\right]\cup\left[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}\right].

(329)

To proceed, we need the following distance formula for matrices expanded in the Pauli basis.

Lemma 21

(Matrix distance in the Pauli basis)
.

For real vectors
\beta=\left[\beta_{a}\ \beta_{b}\ \beta_{c}\ \beta_{d}\right]
and
\gamma=\left[\gamma_{a}\ \gamma_{b}\ \gamma_{c}\ \gamma_{d}\right]
,

\left\lVert\left(\beta_{a}I+i\beta_{b}Z+i\beta_{c}X+i\beta_{d}Y\right)-\left(\gamma_{a}I+i\gamma_{b}Z+i\gamma_{c}X+i\gamma_{d}Y\right)\right\rVert=\left\lVert\beta-\gamma\right\rVert.

(330)

Proof.

We know that the Hermitian matrix

(\beta_{b}-\gamma_{b})Z+(\beta_{c}-\gamma_{c})X+(\beta_{d}-\gamma_{d})Y

(331)

has eigenvalues

\pm\sqrt{(\beta_{b}-\gamma_{b})^{2}+(\beta_{c}-\gamma_{c})^{2}+(\beta_{d}-\gamma_{d})^{2}}.

(332)

The claim then follows by rescaling by
i
and shifting by
\beta_{a}-\gamma_{a}
.
∎

When
\theta\in\left[\frac{\pi}{3},\frac{2\pi}{3}\right]
, our ideal operator is
iX
, so the error is bounded by

\displaystyle\left\lVert iX-\left(\left(f_{a,2}(\theta)I+if_{b,2}(\theta)Z+if_{c,2}(\theta)X+if_{d,2}(\theta)Y\right)\right)\right\rVert

(333)

\displaystyle\leq\ \sqrt{(1-f_{c,2}(\theta))^{2}+f_{a,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d,2}^{2}(\theta)}

\displaystyle\leq\ \sqrt{\epsilon^{2}+2\epsilon-\epsilon^{2}}=\sqrt{2\epsilon}.

Similarly, when
\theta\in\left[-\frac{2\pi}{3},-\frac{\pi}{3}\right]
, our ideal operator is
-iX
, so the error is again bounded by

\displaystyle\left\lVert-iX-\left(\left(f_{a,2}(\theta)I+if_{b,2}(\theta)Z+if_{c,2}(\theta)X+if_{d,2}(\theta)Y\right)\right)\right\rVert

(334)

\displaystyle\leq\ \sqrt{(-1-f_{c,2}(\theta))^{2}+f_{a,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d,2}^{2}(\theta)}

\displaystyle\leq\ \sqrt{\epsilon^{2}+2\epsilon-\epsilon^{2}}=\sqrt{2\epsilon}.

This establishes the following:

Proposition 22

(Branch marking)
.

Let
A/\alpha_{A}
be block encoded by
O_{A}
with normalization factor
\alpha_{A}\geq 2\left\lVert A\right\rVert
.
Let
A|\phi_{u}\rangle=\lambda_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle
and
W|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle=e^{\pm i\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)}|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle
be the corresponding eigenpairs of
A
and the quantum walk operator
W
.
For any
\epsilon>0
, the isometry

|+\rangle|+\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\mapsto|\xi_{u,\pm}\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle,\qquad\left\lVert|\xi_{u,\pm}\rangle-|+\rangle|\pm\rangle\right\rVert\leq\epsilon

(335)

can be implemented using

\mathbf{O}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)\right)

(336)

queries to
O_{A}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma
20
and the proceeding analysis, we can implement the operator

V=\sum_{u}\left(F(\theta_{u,+})\otimes|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y+}|+F(\theta_{u,-})\otimes|\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y-}|\right),

(337)

such that
\left\lVert F(\theta_{u,\pm})-\left(\pm iX\right)\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{2\epsilon}
for all
u
.
Then, the controlled unitary

|0\rangle\!\langle 0|\otimes I+|1\rangle\!\langle 1|\otimes(-iV)

(338)

implements the mapping

|+\rangle|+\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\mapsto|\pm\rangle|+\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle

(339)

to accuracy
\sqrt{2\epsilon}
. The query complexity follows now from
Eq.

311
and the rescaling
\sqrt{2\epsilon}\mapsto\epsilon
.
∎

D.3
Branch marked gapped phase estimation

In GPE, our goal is to distinguish between eigenvalues of the input matrix within intervals

\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in\left[-\frac{\gamma}{\rho},\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in\left(-1,-\gamma\right]\cup\left[\gamma,1\right).

(340)

When we construct the quantum walk operator, each eigenvalue is split into two eigenphases. For the positive branch, our goal is to distinguish between

\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)\in\left[\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right),\pi-\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right)\right]

(341)

and

\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)\in\left(0,\arccos(\gamma)\right]\cup\left[\pi-\arccos(\gamma),\pi\right),

(342)

whereas for the negative branch, we need to differentiate

-\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)\in\left[-\pi+\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right),-\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right)\right]

(343)

and

-\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)\in\left(-\pi,-\pi+\arccos(\gamma)\right]\cup\left[-\arccos(\gamma),0\right).

(344)

Additionally, we need to ensure that GPE has exactly the same behavior on eigenphases with opposite signs.

Let us consider the positive branch first (corresponding to
\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)
). To this end, we choose

\theta_{0}=\frac{\arccos\left(\gamma\right)+\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right)}{2},\qquad\varphi=\frac{\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right)-\arccos\left(\gamma\right)}{2}.

(345)

Then, we implement

\sum_{u}\left(f_{a}(\theta_{u,+})I+if_{b}(\theta_{u,+})Z+if_{c}(\theta_{u,+})X+if_{d}(\theta_{u,+})Y\right)\otimes|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y+}|,

(346)

where

f_{c}(\theta)\approx\begin{cases}0,\quad&\theta\in\left(0,\arccos(\gamma)\right],\\
1,\quad&\theta\in\left[\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right),\pi-\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right)\right],\\
0,\quad&\theta\in\left[\pi-\arccos(\gamma),\pi\right),\\
\end{cases}

(347)

and

f_{a}(\theta)\approx\begin{cases}1,\quad&\theta\in\left(0,\arccos(\gamma)\right],\\
0,\quad&\theta\in\left[\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right),\pi-\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right)\right],\\
-1,\quad&\theta\in\left[\pi-\arccos(\gamma),\pi\right).\\
\end{cases}.

(348)

Note that
f_{a}(\theta_{u,+})\approx\pm 1
has different signs for eigenvalue of the input matrix
\lambda_{u}\gtrless 0
with opposite signs, and the function values are also quite different in the transition band. But this is acceptable since this auxiliary information will be uncomputed in the end.

However, this choice of functions will have a different behavior on the negative branch. To address this issue, we instead aim to perform

\sum_{v}\left(f_{a}(-\theta_{u,-})I+if_{b}(-\theta_{u,-})Z+if_{c}(-\theta_{u,-})X+if_{d}(-\theta_{u,-})Y\right)\otimes|\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y-}|.

(349)

That is, the action of this new operator on negative eigenphases is exactly the same as that of the above operator on positive eigenphases. Moreover, the new polynomials automatically satisfy the requirements. In particular, it holds that
f_{c}(-\theta)=-f_{c}(\theta)
,
f_{a}(-\theta)=f_{a}(\theta)
.

Now we discuss error analysis. Without loss of generality, we focus on the distinguishment of
\left[0,\arccos\left(\gamma\right)\right]
and
\left[\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right),\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
, since the other cases have exactly the same error by symmetry. Proceeding as in the previous subsection, we consider
f_{c}(\theta)=\frac{g(\theta-\theta_{0})-g(-\theta-\theta_{0})}{2}
and
f_{a}(\theta)=\frac{-g(\theta-\theta_{0})-g(-\theta-\theta_{0})}{2}
where

f_{c}^{2}(\theta)+f_{a}^{2}(\theta)=\frac{g^{2}(\theta-\theta_{0})+g^{2}(-\theta-\theta_{0})}{2}.

(350)

We also know from
Table
5
that
f_{c}^{2}(\theta)+f_{a}^{2}(\theta)\leq 1
and
1-\epsilon\leq f_{a}(0)\leq 1
. Furthermore, we have
-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq f_{c}(\theta)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
and
1-\epsilon\leq f_{a}(\theta)\leq 1
for the target interval
0\leq\theta\leq\arccos\left(\gamma\right)
, and
1-\epsilon\leq f_{c}(\theta)\leq 1
and
-\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq f_{a}(\theta)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}
for the target interval
\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right)\leq\theta\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
.
To summarize,

\begin{cases}f_{a}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c}^{2}(\theta)\leq 1,\quad&\forall\theta\in[-\pi,\pi],\\
1-\epsilon\leq f_{a}(0)\leq 1,\\
\left\lvert f_{c}(\theta)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2},\ 1-\epsilon\leq f_{a}(\theta)\leq 1,&\forall\theta\in\left[0,\arccos\left(\gamma\right)\right],\\
1-\epsilon\leq f_{c}(\theta)\leq 1,\ \left\lvert f_{a}(\theta)\right\rvert\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2},&\forall\theta\in\left[\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right),\frac{\pi}{2}\right].\end{cases}

(351)

When
\theta\in\left[\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right),\frac{\pi}{2}\right]
, our ideal operator is
iX
, so the error can be bounded in a similar way as in the previous subsection

\displaystyle\left\lVert iX-\left(\left(f_{a,2}(\theta)I+if_{b,2}(\theta)Z+if_{c,2}(\theta)X+if_{d,2}(\theta)Y\right)\right)\right\rVert

(352)

\displaystyle\leq\ \sqrt{(1-f_{c,2}(\theta))^{2}+f_{a,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d,2}^{2}(\theta)}

\displaystyle\leq\ \sqrt{\epsilon^{2}+2\epsilon-\epsilon^{2}}=\sqrt{2\epsilon}.

Let us now consider
\theta\in\left[0,\arccos\left(\gamma\right)\right]
. We let

f_{a,1}(\theta)=f_{a}(\theta),\qquad f_{c,1}(\theta)=f_{c}(\theta),

(353)

and run the sum-of-squares method
[
41
]
to obtain

\begin{cases}f_{a,1}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,1}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c,1}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d,1}^{2}(\theta)=1,\quad&\forall\theta\in[-\pi,\pi],\\
f_{a,1}^{2}(0)+f_{b,1}^{2}(0)=1.\end{cases}

(354)

Continuing, we define

f_{a,2}(\theta)=f_{a,1}(\theta)f_{a,1}(0)+f_{b,1}(\theta)f_{b,1}(0),\qquad f_{c,2}(\theta)=f_{c,1}(\theta).

(355)

and rerun the sum-of-squares method to get

\begin{cases}f_{a,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{b,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d,2}^{2}(\theta)=1,\quad&\forall\theta\in[-\pi,\pi],\\
f_{a,2}(0)=1.\end{cases}

(356)

This results in

\sum_{u}\left(f_{a,2}(\theta_{u,+})I+if_{b,2}(\theta_{u,+})Z+if_{c,2}(\theta_{u,+})X+if_{d,2}(\theta_{u,+})Y\right)\otimes|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y+}|.

(357)

Now we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert f_{a,2}(\theta)-f_{a,1}(\theta)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert f_{a,1}(\theta)\left(f_{a,1}(0)-1\right)+f_{b,1}(\theta)f_{b,1}(0)\right\rvert

(358)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert f_{a,1}(\theta)\right\rvert\left\lvert f_{a,1}(0)-1\right\rvert+\sqrt{1-f_{a,1}^{2}(\theta)}\sqrt{1-f_{a,1}^{2}(0)}

\displaystyle\leq 1\cdot\epsilon+\sqrt{1-(1-\epsilon)^{2}}\sqrt{1-(1-\epsilon)^{2}}=3\epsilon-\epsilon^{2},

which implies

\left\lvert f_{a,2}(\theta)-1\right\rvert\leq 4\epsilon-\epsilon^{2}.

(359)

Combining with the obvious requirement that
\left\lvert f_{a,2}\right\rvert\leq 1
, we arrive at

1-4\epsilon\leq 1-4\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}\leq f_{a,2}(\theta)\leq 1.

(360)

When
\theta\in\left[0,\arccos\left(\gamma\right)\right]
, our ideal operator is
I
, so the error can be bounded as

\displaystyle\left\lVert I-\left(\left(f_{a,2}(\theta)I+if_{b,2}(\theta)Z+if_{c,2}(\theta)X+if_{d,2}(\theta)Y\right)\right)\right\rVert

(361)

\displaystyle\leq\ \sqrt{(1-f_{a,2}(\theta))^{2}+f_{b,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{c,2}^{2}(\theta)+f_{d,2}^{2}(\theta)}

\displaystyle\leq\ \sqrt{(4\epsilon)^{2}+1-(1-4\epsilon)^{2}}=2\sqrt{2\epsilon}.

Altogether, we have established the following proposition. Note it is important that the output state
|\xi_{u}\rangle
of gapped phase estimation has
no
dependence on the
\pm
branches of eigenphases. This is the primary goal of branch marking.

Proposition 23

(Branch marked gapped phase estimation)
.

Let
A/\alpha_{A}
be block encoded by
O_{A}
with normalization factor
\alpha_{A}\geq 2\left\lVert A\right\rVert
.
Let
A|\phi_{u}\rangle=\lambda_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle
and
W|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle=e^{\pm i\arccos\left(\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right)}|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle
be the corresponding eigenpairs of
A
and the quantum walk operator
W
.
For any
\epsilon>0
,
0<\gamma<\frac{\alpha_{A}}{2}
and constant
\rho>0
, the isometry

|\pm\rangle|0\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\mapsto|\pm\rangle|\xi_{u}\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle,\qquad\begin{cases}\left\lVert|\xi_{u}\rangle-|0\rangle\right\rVert\leq\epsilon,\quad&\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in[\gamma,1),\\
\left\lVert|\xi_{u}\rangle-i|1\rangle\right\rVert\leq\epsilon,&\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in\left[-\frac{\gamma}{\rho},\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right],\\
\left\lVert|\xi_{u}\rangle-(-|0\rangle)\right\rVert\leq\epsilon,&\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in\left(-1,-\gamma\right],\end{cases}

(362)

can be implemented
using

\mathbf{O}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}\log\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)\right)

(363)

queries to
O_{A}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma
20
and the proceeding analysis, we can implement the operators

V_{+}=\sum_{u}F(\theta_{u,+})\otimes|\phi_{u,Y+}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y+}|,\qquad V_{-}=\sum_{u}F(-\theta_{u,-})\otimes|\phi_{u,Y-}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u,Y-}|

(364)

respectively, such that

\begin{cases}\left\lVert F(\theta)-I\right\rVert\leq 2\sqrt{2\epsilon},\quad&\theta\in\left(0,\arccos\left(\gamma\right)\right],\\
\left\lVert F(\theta)-iX\right\rVert\leq\sqrt{2\epsilon},&\theta\in\left[\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right),\pi-\arccos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right)\right],\\
\left\lVert F(\theta)-(-I)\right\rVert\leq 2\sqrt{2\epsilon},&\theta\in\left[\pi-\arccos(\gamma),\pi\right).\\
\end{cases}

(365)

Then, the controlled unitary

|+\rangle\!\langle+|\otimes V_{+}+|-\rangle\!\langle-|\otimes V_{-}

(366)

implements the mapping

|\pm\rangle|0\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle\mapsto\begin{cases}|\pm\rangle|0\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle,\quad&\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in[\gamma,1),\\
i|\pm\rangle|1\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle,&\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in\left[-\frac{\gamma}{\rho},\frac{\gamma}{\rho}\right],\\
-|\pm\rangle|0\rangle|\phi_{u,Y\pm}\rangle,&\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\in\left(-1,-\gamma\right],\\
\end{cases}

(367)

to accuracy
2\sqrt{2\epsilon}
. The query complexity follows now from
Eq.

311
and the rescaling
2\sqrt{2\epsilon}\mapsto\epsilon
.
∎

Appendix E
Multiplicative approximation of success probabilities

In this appendix, we will consider a number of success probabilities involved in the analysis of our quantum linear system algorithm in
Section
4
. Specifically, we will analyze five different probabilities and show that they are in fact all constant multiplicative approximations of one another.

E.1
Definition of success probabilities

The first probability corresponds to the success probability of applying a block-encoded inverse of the input matrix to the initial state. Suppose that our input matrix is block encoded as
A/\alpha_{A}
with normalization factor
\alpha_{A}\geq\left\lVert A\right\rVert
and upper bound
\alpha_{A^{-1}}\geq\left\lVert A^{-1}\right\rVert
on norm of the inverse matrix. Without loss of generality, we assume that both
\alpha_{A}
and
\alpha_{A^{-1}}
are powers of
3
, so that
\alpha_{A}\alpha_{A^{-1}}=3^{m}
for some positive integer
m
.
By switching to the Hermitian dilation
|0\rangle\!\langle 1|\otimes A+|1\rangle\!\langle 0|\otimes A^{\dagger}
, we further assume that
A
is Hermitian with the spectral decomposition
A=\sum_{u}\lambda_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{u}|
where
\frac{1}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}}\leq\left\lvert\lambda_{u}\right\rvert\leq\alpha_{A}
, while the initial state can be expanded in the eigenbasis of
A
as
|b\rangle=\sum_{u}\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle
.
Then, we define

\psi_{\text{inv}}=\frac{A^{-1}}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}}|b\rangle=\sum_{u}\frac{1}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}\lambda_{u}}\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,\qquad p_{\text{succ}}=\frac{\left\lVert A^{-1}|b\rangle\right\rVert^{2}}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}^{2}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}^{2}}\left(\sum_{u}\left\lvert\frac{\gamma_{u}}{\lambda_{u}}\right\rvert^{2}\right).

(368)

The second probability is an auxiliary probability for a version of discretized inverse state supported only on one basis state of the time register. We let

\psi_{\text{d-inv},1}=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\frac{3^{k+1}}{3^{m}}|k\rangle\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,\qquad p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}.

(369)

The third probability corresponds to the probability of the discretized inverse state supported on two basis states of the time register. Concretely,

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{d-inv}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left(\zeta_{k+1,u}\frac{3^{k+1}}{3^{m}}|k\rangle+\zeta_{k,u}\frac{3^{k}}{3^{m}}|k-1\rangle\right)\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,

(370)

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}.

The fourth probability is also an auxiliary probability for a version of discretized inverse state with full support on all basis states of the time register, assuming branch marking is performed perfectly:

\displaystyle\psi_{\text{d-inv},m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\zeta_{j,u}\frac{3^{j}}{3^{m}}|j-1\rangle\gamma_{u}|\phi_{u}\rangle,

(371)

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}.

Finally, the fifth probability is for the actual state
\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}
prepared by the erroneous GPE and branch marking. We denote this probability by
p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}
.

E.2
Multiplicative bounds on success probabilities

We now give multiplicative bounds on the success probabilities introduced in the previous subsection. Our analysis uses the following lemma.

Lemma 24
.

The following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j\neq k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+2}}{9^{m}}\right)\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2},

(372)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j\neq k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\right)\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2}.

Proof.

Recall that the cumulative coefficients
\zeta_{j,u}
have the following bound

\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert\leq\begin{cases}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j},\qquad&j\leq k-1,\\
\prod\limits_{l=k+1}^{j-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},l},&j\geq k+2.\end{cases}

(373)

Therefore, we can prove the first claim as

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}+\sum_{j=k+2}^{m}\prod_{l=k+1}^{j-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},l}^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}\right)

(374)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}^{2}\frac{9^{k-1}}{9^{m}}+\sum_{j=k+2}^{m}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j-1}^{2}\frac{9^{k+2}}{9^{m}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}^{2}\frac{9^{k+2}}{9^{m}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+2}}{9^{m}}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}\right)^{2}}_{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2}},

where we have assumed all
\epsilon_{\text{gpe},l}\leq\frac{1}{3}
.
Similarly for the second claim,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}+\sum_{j=k+2}^{m}\prod_{l=k+1}^{j-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},l}^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\right)

(375)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}+\sum_{j=k+2}^{m}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j-1}^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}\underbrace{\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe},j}\right)^{2}}_{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2}}.

∎

Let us begin with the relation between
p_{\text{succ}}
and
p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}
which is easy to describe. Following

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\frac{\gamma_{u}}{\lambda_{u}}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{\alpha_{A}^{2}}=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}},

(376)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\frac{\gamma_{u}}{\lambda_{u}}\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\alpha_{A^{-1}}^{2}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{\alpha_{A}^{2}}=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}},

we conclude that

\frac{p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}}{9}\leq p_{\text{succ}}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}.

(377)

Then, we consider
p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}
and
p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}
. It is fairly straightforward to derive one side of the bound

\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k+1}}{9^{m}}.

(378)

Analysis of the other side however is more involved and relies on
Lemma
24
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}\geq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}

(379)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}-\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j\neq k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{k}}{9^{m}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}}{9}-\frac{p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}}{9}\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2}.

This gives

\frac{p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}}{9}\left(1-\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2}\right)\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}.

(380)

Next, we discuss
p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}
and
p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}
. Again, one side of the bound follows trivially from the definition

\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}.

(381)

As for the other side, we apply
Lemma
24
to compute

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}

(382)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}-\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{j\neq k,k+1}\left\lvert\zeta_{j,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{j}}{9^{m}}

\displaystyle\geq p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}-9^{2}p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2}\geq p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}-9^{3}p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}{1-\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}}\right)^{2}.

We thus obtain

p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\left(1+9^{3}\frac{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2}}{\left(1-\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}\right)^{2}}\right).

(383)

Finally, we examine
p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}
and
p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}
. As branch marking is invoked twice, we have the additive approximation

\left\lvert p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}-p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}\right\rvert\leq 2\left\lvert\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}}-\sqrt{p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}}\right\rvert\leq 4\epsilon_{\text{bm}}.

(384)

Let us summarize these estimates as follows.

Proposition 25

(Multiplicative bounds on success probabilities)
.

Let the five success probabilities
p_{\text{succ}}
,
p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}
,
p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}
,
p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}
and
p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}
be defined as in
Appendix
E.1
. Then,

\displaystyle\frac{p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}}{9}\leq

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ}}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1},

(385)

\displaystyle\frac{p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1}}{9}\left(1-\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2}\right)\leq

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1},

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\leq

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}\leq p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}\left(1+9^{3}\frac{\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}^{2}}{\left(1-\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}\right)^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle\left\lvert p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}-p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}\right\rvert\leq 4\epsilon_{\text{bm}}.

Hence, as long as

\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}=\mathbf{O}(1),\qquad\epsilon_{\text{bm}}=\mathbf{O}\left(p_{\text{succ}}\right),

(386)

all success probabilities are constant multiplicative approximations of one another

p_{\text{succ,d-inv},1},\ p_{\text{succ,d-inv}},\ p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m},\ p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ}}\right).

(387)

E.3
Multiplicative bounds on sum of amplification thresholds

In
Section
4.2
, we have derived the following multiplicative bounds on sum of amplification thresholds

p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}9^{l}\leq\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}\leq\frac{5}{4}p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}9^{l},

(388)

where we assume VTAA with input algorithms
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
produces
\psi_{\text{d-inv}}
. We now incorporate the error of GPE and branch marking into the analysis.

When we consider erroneous GPE and perfect branch marking, VTAA with input algorithms
B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}
produces
\psi_{\text{d-inv},m}
and the potentially good probabilities take the form

\displaystyle\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}B_{j}\cdots B_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{h=j+1}^{m}\left\lvert\zeta_{h,u}\right\rvert^{2}

(389)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{h=1}^{j}\left\lvert\zeta_{h,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{h}}{9^{m}}.

Correspondingly, the sum of amplification thresholds are split into two terms:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}B_{j}\cdots B_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{h=j+1}^{m}\left\lvert\zeta_{h,u}\right\rvert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

(390)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{h=1}^{j}\left\lvert\zeta_{h,u}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{9^{h}}{9^{m}}9^{j-m+l}.

We exchange the summation order in the first term to get

\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\sum_{h=j+1}^{m}9^{j}=\sum_{h=m-l+2}^{m}\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{h-1}9^{j}=\frac{9}{8}\sum_{h=m-l+2}^{m}9^{h-1},

(391)

whereas for the second term

\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\sum_{h=1}^{j}9^{j}=\sum_{h=1}^{m}\sum_{j=h}^{m}9^{j}=\frac{9}{8}\sum_{h=1}^{m}9^{m}.

(392)

Altogether,

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}9^{l}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}B_{j}\cdots B_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

(393)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{4}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{\left\lvert\frac{\lambda_{u}}{\alpha_{A}}\right\rvert\in\big[\frac{1}{3^{k+1}},\frac{1}{3^{k}}\big)}\left\lvert\gamma_{u}\right\rvert^{2}\sum_{h=1}^{m}\left\lvert\zeta_{h,u}\right\rvert^{2}9^{h-m+l}\leq\frac{5}{4}p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}9^{l}.

Finally, we consider the general case where both GPE and branch marking are imperfect. In this case, VTAA with input algorithms
C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}
produces
\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}
with
\left\lVert\psi_{\text{d-inv,bm}}\right\rVert^{2}=p_{\text{succ,d-inv,bm}}
. Then, the sum of amplification thresholds has error

\left\lvert\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}B_{j}\cdots B_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}-\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}C_{j}\cdots C_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}\right\rvert\leq\frac{9}{8}4\epsilon_{\text{bm}}9^{l}.

(394)

We thus obtain:

Proposition 26

(Multiplicative bounds on sum of amplification thresholds)
.

Let the input algorithms
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
,
B_{1},\ldots,B_{m}
and
C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}
be defined as in
Appendix
E.3
. Then,

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}9^{l}\leq\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{4}p_{\text{succ,d-inv}}9^{l},

(395)

\displaystyle p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}9^{l}\leq\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}B_{j}\cdots B_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{5}{4}p_{\text{succ,d-inv},m}9^{l},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}B_{j}\cdots B_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}-\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}C_{j}\cdots C_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{9}{8}4\epsilon_{\text{bm}}9^{l}.

Hence, as long as

\epsilon_{\text{gpe}}=\mathbf{O}(1),\qquad\epsilon_{\text{bm}}=\mathbf{O}\left(p_{\text{succ}}\right),

(396)

all sums of amplification thresholds have the scaling

\displaystyle\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}A_{j}\cdots A_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ}}9^{l}\right),

(397)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}B_{j}\cdots B_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ}}9^{l}\right),

\displaystyle\sum_{j=m-l+1}^{m}\left\lVert\overline{\Pi_{j}\Pi_{b}}C_{j}\cdots C_{1}|\psi_{0}\rangle\right\rVert^{2}9^{j-m+l}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Theta}\left(p_{\text{succ}}9^{l}\right).
